03Qualifying a bank reconciliation differenceA July posting helps explain June+

Your goal

Describe a plausible timing difference with the correct direction and dates while keeping the broader reconciliation incomplete.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

A July posting helps explain June

A fictional bank reconciliation runs through June 30. The bank balance is $300 lower than the cash ledger. A $300 deposit entered in the ledger on June 30 appears in the bank's July 1 activity. Other reconciliation checks are not complete. Bookkeeper Nia explains the timing evidence to colleague Omar without claiming that every difference has been cleared or that an adjusting entry is automatically needed.

Omar: Finance colleague / Nia: Bookkeeper

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which balance is lower at June 30?

Answer and explanation

A. The bank balance by $300

The brief explicitly states that the bank balance is three hundred dollars below the ledger.

2. When does the deposit appear in bank activity?

Answer and explanation

B. July 1

The deposit is in the June 30 ledger but appears in bank activity on July 1.

3. What is the status of the broader reconciliation?

Answer and explanation

C. Other checks remain incomplete

The supplied facts leave other reconciliation checks unfinished.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

At the June 30 cutoff, the bank is $300 lower than the ledger. A $300 deposit recorded in the ledger on June 30 appears at the bank on July 1. That sequence is consistent with a timing difference in the stated direction.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
bank reconciliation
Comparison explaining differences between bank and accounting records.prepare a bank reconciliation
cash ledger
Accounting record containing the relevant cash transactions.compare the cash ledger
bank balance
Amount reported in the relevant bank record.identify the bank balance
ledger balance
Amount shown in the corresponding accounting record.distinguish the ledger balance
reconciliation cutoff
Date through which the comparison is being prepared.state the reconciliation cutoff
deposit
Money placed into the relevant bank account.trace the deposit
deposit in transit
Deposit recorded by the business but not yet recorded by the bank at the comparison date.identify a deposit in transit
timing difference
Mismatch caused by the same item appearing at different times.explain a timing difference
bank activity
Transactions appearing in the bank's record.check subsequent bank activity
posting date
Date on which an entry is recorded in a system.compare posting dates
subsequent activity
Transactions recorded after the period being reviewed.inspect subsequent activity
reconciling item
Item explaining part of a difference between records.document a reconciling item
outstanding check
Issued check not yet reflected as cleared in the relevant bank record.distinguish an outstanding check
bank charge
Fee recorded by the bank for a relevant service.identify a bank charge
interest credit
Amount added by the bank for earned interest.distinguish an interest credit
recording error
Incorrect entry of a transaction or amount.investigate a recording error
adjusted balance
Balance after the relevant reconciliation adjustments are considered.verify the adjusted balance
unreconciled difference
Portion of a discrepancy not yet fully resolved.report an unreconciled difference
reconciliation evidence
Records supporting the comparison and explanation.retain reconciliation evidence
direction of difference
Which figure is higher or lower and by how much.state the direction of difference
completion claim
Statement that the whole review has been finished.qualify a completion claim
review status
Current stage of the checking process.preserve review status
ledger adjustment
Change posted to the accounting record through the relevant process.distinguish a ledger adjustment
reconciliation sign-off
Confirmation of the completed review by the relevant person.obtain reconciliation sign-off

The deposit is already recorded in the ledger, so do not propose recording it again merely to explain the timing. Other reconciliation checks are unfinished. The matching amount supports the explanation but does not establish a complete reconciliation or approved adjustment.

C Notice the language

Explain a plausible cause without declaring the review finished

A matching amount and sensible timing can support an explanation while other checks remain open. Name the cutoff, the direction of the difference, and both posting dates. A careful summary can be useful without overstating the completeness of the reconciliation.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the cutoff
This reconciliation is through June 30.
State the direction
The bank balance is $300 lower than the cash ledger.
Identify the ledger entry
The deposit was recorded in the ledger on June 30.
Identify the bank date
It appears in the bank activity on July 1.
Explain the timing
The same deposit appears on opposite sides of the cutoff.
Qualify the explanation
That is consistent with a timing difference.
Use the technical term
It is a deposit-in-transit item at the comparison date.
Avoid reversing the difference
The bank is lower, not higher, at June 30.
Keep the receipt distinct
The ledger already contains the deposit.
Avoid duplicate recording
We should not record the same deposit again merely for timing.
Preserve the open checks
Other reconciliation checks are not complete.
Limit the conclusion
This explains a plausible item, not the whole review.
Request the evidence
Keep both dated records with the reconciliation.
Separate correction and timing
A timing difference is not automatically a recording error.
Avoid premature sign-off
I cannot call the reconciliation complete yet.
Close with a qualified summary
The deposit supports the timing explanation; the remaining checks are still open.
6 language and precision notes
Through June 30
Defines the comparison cutoff rather than a later bank date.
Lower than
Preserves the direction of the stated difference.
On July 1
Gives the subsequent bank appearance of the deposit.
Consistent with
Signals supported interpretation without claiming every check is complete.
Already contains
Explains why blindly adding the same deposit again would be misleading.
Not complete yet
Keeps the broader review state explicit.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which explanation has the correct direction?

Answer and explanation

D. The June 30 ledger includes $300 that appears at the bank July 1

The earlier ledger entry is consistent with the bank being lower at the June cutoff.

2. Why should the deposit not simply be entered again?

Answer and explanation

A. It is already recorded in the cash ledger

The ledger already contains the deposit, so timing alone does not justify recording it again.

3. Which completion statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. The timing item is supported, but other checks remain open

The brief leaves other checks incomplete despite the matching deposit evidence.

4. Which evidence supports this cutoff explanation?

Answer and explanation

C. The June 30 ledger deposit linked to its July 1 bank appearance

The two linked dated records explain when the same deposit appears on each side; neither a duplicate posting nor an undated balance claim supplies that evidence.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

A July posting helps explain June

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Omar: Finance colleague / Nia: Bookkeeper

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • deposit in transit
  • cash ledger
  • Reconciliation sign-off
  • bank activity
  • ledger adjustment
  • reconciliation cutoff
  • review status
  • direction of difference
  • timing difference
  • reconciling item
  1. Omar
    The June summary has the bank three hundred below the ledger. Have you found an explanation, or are we still looking for the missing amount?
  2. Nia
    There's evidence of a 1. A $300 deposit is in the ledger on June 30 and appears in bank activity on July 1.
  3. Omar
    So at the June cutoff the ledger includes it, but the bank doesn't show it until the next day.
  4. Nia
    Right. Our 2 is June 30. The sequence fits the bank being lower by $300, not higher.
  5. Omar
    Is deposit in transit the right wording? I don't want the manager to read missing deposit as money the bank has lost.
  6. Nia
    Yes, the dated records support a 3 at that comparison date. Its appearance on July 1 supports the timing explanation.
  7. Omar
    It matches the entire difference. Can I mark the reconciliation complete now?
  8. Nia
    Not yet. The 4 still includes unfinished checks. One matching amount doesn't show that we've reviewed every other transaction.
  9. Omar
    Then the update should say we've found supporting evidence, while leaving the rest of the review open.
  10. Nia
    Exactly. The 5 is supported by the two dated records. Keep that progress separate from a claim that all checks are finished.
  11. Omar
    Someone suggested adding another three hundred to the ledger so the records agree. Would that be the adjustment?
  12. Nia
    No. The 6 already includes this deposit. Recording it again merely because the bank shows it later would duplicate the same receipt.
  13. Omar
    So we first distinguish a timing item from an unrecorded transaction. Not every difference calls for another entry.
  14. Nia
    Correct. These facts don't establish a 7. We are explaining the existing deposit, not authorizing a duplicate posting.
  15. Omar
    What should stay with the reconciliation so another reviewer can verify the explanation?
  16. Nia
    Keep the June 30 ledger entry and July 1 8 together, with the amount and their connection visible.
  17. Omar
    I'll write bank lower by $300 at June 30, matching deposit in the bank the next day, other checks unfinished.
  18. Nia
    That preserves the 9 and the review limit. It doesn't hide unfinished work behind the word balanced.
  19. Omar
    And I won't say approved. The evidence is progress, but the remaining checks still have to be completed.
  20. Nia
    Agreed. 10 is outstanding. Keep the supported timing explanation in the working file for the rest of the review.

Answers and explanations
  1. timing difference Turn 2. Timing difference is supported by the deposit appearing in the June 30 ledger and July 1 bank activity.
  2. reconciliation cutoff Turn 4. Reconciliation cutoff is June 30, so the July 1 bank entry belongs to subsequent activity.
  3. deposit in transit Turn 6. Deposit in transit describes the deposit recorded in the books but not yet in the bank record at the cutoff.
  4. review status Turn 8. Review status remains incomplete because other reconciliation checks have not been finished.
  5. reconciling item Turn 10. Reconciling item is the identified deposit timing difference, not proof that the entire review is complete.
  6. cash ledger Turn 12. Cash ledger already includes the June 30 deposit, so timing alone does not justify entering the same deposit again.
  7. ledger adjustment Turn 14. Ledger adjustment is a separate posting decision and is not automatically required by this timing evidence.
  8. bank activity Turn 16. Bank activity on July 1 is the subsequent record supporting the deposit's later bank appearance.
  9. direction of difference Turn 18. Direction of difference is bank below ledger by three hundred dollars, not the reverse.
  10. Reconciliation sign-off Turn 20. Reconciliation sign-off has not been established while other required checks remain unfinished.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Omar: The June summary has the bank three hundred below the ledger. Have you found an explanation, or are we still looking for the missing amount?
  2. Nia: There's evidence of a timing difference. A $300 deposit is in the ledger on June 30 and appears in bank activity on July 1.
  3. Omar: So at the June cutoff the ledger includes it, but the bank doesn't show it until the next day.
  4. Nia: Right. Our reconciliation cutoff is June 30. The sequence fits the bank being lower by $300, not higher.
  5. Omar: Is deposit in transit the right wording? I don't want the manager to read missing deposit as money the bank has lost.
  6. Nia: Yes, the dated records support a deposit in transit at that comparison date. Its appearance on July 1 supports the timing explanation.
  7. Omar: It matches the entire difference. Can I mark the reconciliation complete now?
  8. Nia: Not yet. The review status still includes unfinished checks. One matching amount doesn't show that we've reviewed every other transaction.
  9. Omar: Then the update should say we've found supporting evidence, while leaving the rest of the review open.
  10. Nia: Exactly. The reconciling item is supported by the two dated records. Keep that progress separate from a claim that all checks are finished.
  11. Omar: Someone suggested adding another three hundred to the ledger so the records agree. Would that be the adjustment?
  12. Nia: No. The cash ledger already includes this deposit. Recording it again merely because the bank shows it later would duplicate the same receipt.
  13. Omar: So we first distinguish a timing item from an unrecorded transaction. Not every difference calls for another entry.
  14. Nia: Correct. These facts don't establish a ledger adjustment. We are explaining the existing deposit, not authorizing a duplicate posting.
  15. Omar: What should stay with the reconciliation so another reviewer can verify the explanation?
  16. Nia: Keep the June 30 ledger entry and July 1 bank activity together, with the amount and their connection visible.
  17. Omar: I'll write bank lower by $300 at June 30, matching deposit in the bank the next day, other checks unfinished.
  18. Nia: That preserves the direction of difference and the review limit. It doesn't hide unfinished work behind the word balanced.
  19. Omar: And I won't say approved. The evidence is progress, but the remaining checks still have to be completed.
  20. Nia: Agreed. Reconciliation sign-off is outstanding. Keep the supported timing explanation in the working file for the rest of the review.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A deposit appears one day later

A fictional reviewer discusses the June 30 reconciliation with the bookkeeper.

  1. Reviewer: The bank balance is three hundred lower. Have you found anything that explains that difference?
  2. Bookkeeper: Yes. That deposit in transit appears in the bank's July first activity for three hundred.
  3. Reviewer: Does the reference match, or are we relying only on two amounts that happen to agree?
  4. Bookkeeper: The deposit reference matches as well. That supports a timing explanation for this particular item.
  5. Reviewer: Then can I mark the account reconciled and move it to the completed folder?
  6. Bookkeeper: Not yet. The deposit is traced, but the other reconciliation checks are still open.
  7. Reviewer: Which wording separates that progress from the overall status without making it sound like nothing happened?
  8. Bookkeeper: Deposit timing confirmed for this item; the remaining account checks are pending.
  9. Reviewer: That works. Keep the July first reference with the June reconciliation so the reviewer can follow it.
  10. Bookkeeper: I've linked it and kept the overall status open. The evidence supports the item, not completion.
Equal totals with an unmatched pair

A fictional reconciliation review finds two equal and opposite unexplained amounts.

  1. Bookkeeper: The totals agree, but I still have an unmatched forty-dollar receipt and forty-dollar payment.
  2. Reviewer: Could those simply cancel out, leaving us with no reconciliation work to do?
  3. Bookkeeper: They offset numerically, but they may relate to different transactions. We haven't identified either one.
  4. Reviewer: What would be misleading about calling the difference zero and closing both questions now?
  5. Bookkeeper: Zero describes the net total. It doesn't establish that the individual entries are correctly recorded.
  6. Reviewer: Understood. Can you show them as two separate reconciling items rather than one net line?
  7. Bookkeeper: Yes. That preserves each reference, direction, and open question for the person tracing them.
  8. Reviewer: I'll take the receipt query. Could you ask the payments clerk about the outgoing forty?
  9. Bookkeeper: I will, and neither item will be labeled cleared merely because the amounts are equal.
  10. Reviewer: Then our summary is totals agree, two unmatched items remain. That's a useful qualified result.
A bank fee or a look-alike amount

A fictional clerk checks an unexplained bank debit with a senior bookkeeper.

  1. Clerk: There's a fifteen-dollar bank debit without a matching ledger entry. Last month's fee was fifteen too.
  2. Senior bookkeeper: That makes a fee plausible, but does this statement actually describe the debit as a fee?
  3. Clerk: No. It shows a short reference that I haven't yet matched to a transaction description.
  4. Senior bookkeeper: Then we should call it an unidentified debit rather than repeat last month's explanation as fact.
  5. Clerk: The detailed statement view is available. Would its description be useful evidence for the query?
  6. Senior bookkeeper: Yes, use that reference. We need to establish what this debit represents before discussing any entry.
  7. Clerk: I've opened it now. It identifies a returned transfer charge, rather than the monthly account fee.
  8. Senior bookkeeper: That narrows the explanation. The responsible reviewer still needs to confirm its handling in our records.
  9. Clerk: I'll replace the guessed monthly-fee label with the statement's description and supporting reference.
  10. Senior bookkeeper: We've identified the bank description; the ledger review remains incomplete.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A July posting helps explain June
  1. Complete the reconciliation dialogue.
  2. Read the cutoff explanation aloud: June 30 ledger, July 1 bank, bank $300 lower at June 30. Then read why a second ledger deposit is not justified.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Explain the cutoff difference precisely

Complete the reconciliation summary using the supplied direction, amount, dates, and review status.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Bookkeeper: "At June 30, the bank is $300 ___ than the ledger."

Answer and explanation

A. lower

Lower preserves the stated direction of the difference at the cutoff.

2. Colleague: "The deposit is recorded in the ledger on June ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 30

June 30 is the ledger date, before the deposit appears in bank activity.

3. Bookkeeper: "It appears at the bank on July ___."

Answer and explanation

B. 1

July 1 is the subsequent bank date supporting the timing explanation.

4. Colleague: "Other reconciliation checks remain ___."

Answer and explanation

C. incomplete

Incomplete prevents the supported timing item from becoming a false claim of full reconciliation.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 03: Qualifying a bank reconciliation difference
Original fictional case: A fictional bank reconciliation runs through June 30. The bank balance is $300 lower than the cash ledger. A $300 deposit entered in the ledger on June 30 appears in the bank's July 1 activity. Other reconciliation checks are not complete. Bookkeeper Nia explains the timing evidence to colleague Omar without claiming that every difference has been cleared or that an adjusting entry is automatically needed.
Language workshop: A July posting helps explain June
Communication goal: Describe a plausible timing difference with the correct direction and dates while keeping the broader reconciliation incomplete.
Language guidance: At the June 30 cutoff, the bank is $300 lower than the ledger. A $300 deposit recorded in the ledger on June 30 appears at the bank on July 1. That sequence is consistent with a timing difference in the stated direction.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): This reconciliation is through June 30. / The bank balance is $300 lower than the cash ledger. / The deposit was recorded in the ledger on June 30. / It appears in the bank activity on July 1. / The same deposit appears on opposite sides of the cutoff. / That is consistent with a timing difference. / It is a deposit-in-transit item at the comparison date. / The bank is lower, not higher, at June 30. / The ledger already contains the deposit. / We should not record the same deposit again merely for timing. / Other reconciliation checks are not complete. / This explains a plausible item, not the whole review. / Keep both dated records with the reconciliation. / A timing difference is not automatically a recording error. / I cannot call the reconciliation complete yet. / The deposit supports the timing explanation; the remaining checks are still open.
Vocabulary: bank reconciliation: Comparison explaining differences between bank and accounting records. / cash ledger: Accounting record containing the relevant cash transactions. / bank balance: Amount reported in the relevant bank record. / ledger balance: Amount shown in the corresponding accounting record. / reconciliation cutoff: Date through which the comparison is being prepared. / deposit: Money placed into the relevant bank account. / deposit in transit: Deposit recorded by the business but not yet recorded by the bank at the comparison date. / timing difference: Mismatch caused by the same item appearing at different times. / bank activity: Transactions appearing in the bank's record. / posting date: Date on which an entry is recorded in a system. / subsequent activity: Transactions recorded after the period being reviewed. / reconciling item: Item explaining part of a difference between records. / outstanding check: Issued check not yet reflected as cleared in the relevant bank record. / bank charge: Fee recorded by the bank for a relevant service. / interest credit: Amount added by the bank for earned interest. / recording error: Incorrect entry of a transaction or amount. / adjusted balance: Balance after the relevant reconciliation adjustments are considered. / unreconciled difference: Portion of a discrepancy not yet fully resolved. / reconciliation evidence: Records supporting the comparison and explanation. / direction of difference: Which figure is higher or lower and by how much. / completion claim: Statement that the whole review has been finished. / review status: Current stage of the checking process. / ledger adjustment: Change posted to the accounting record through the relevant process. / reconciliation sign-off: Confirmation of the completed review by the relevant person.
Speaking task: Complete the reconciliation dialogue. Read the cutoff explanation aloud: June 30 ledger, July 1 bank, bank $300 lower at June 30. Then read why a second ledger deposit is not justified. Complete the transfer and check the key. Retain the unfinished review status in the corrected summary.
Partner: Omar: Finance colleague / Nia: Bookkeeper
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the reconciliation summary using the supplied direction, amount, dates, and review status.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Requesting evidence for time exceptionsA missing clock-out is not a known finish time+

Your goal

Request evidence for a time-record exception while distinguishing the roster, employee recollection, and confirmed actual work time.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

A missing clock-out is not a known finish time

Employee Eli's Tuesday timecard has no clock-out entry. The roster shows a 17:00 finish, while Eli recalls finishing at 17:30. Supervisor Hana can review a work log before the internal approval cutoff at 14:00 today. Payroll assistant Dev must request that review and preserve both sources without choosing a finish time. Actual finish time is unconfirmed. The internal cutoff does not authorize an invented entry or determine Eli's pay rights.

Dev: Payroll assistant / Hana: Supervisor

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is missing from Tuesday's timecard?

Answer and explanation

A. A clock-out entry

The timecard lacks the end-of-work entry, while other information is separately available.

2. How do the two time sources differ?

Answer and explanation

A. Roster says 17:00; Eli recalls 17:30

The roster and employee recollection give different times, neither yet confirmed as actual.

3. What can Hana review before 14:00?

Answer and explanation

A. The work log

The brief identifies the work log as evidence the supervisor can review before the internal cutoff.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The roster gives 17:00, Eli recalls 17:30, and the actual finish remains unconfirmed. Do not convert the scheduled time into verified work time or treat a missing clock-out as proof of no work.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
timecard
Record of employee work-time entries.check the timecard
clock-out
Entry recording the end of a work period.query a missing clock-out
clock-in
Entry recording the start of a work period.verify the clock-in
roster
Planned schedule of assigned work.distinguish the roster
scheduled finish
End time shown in the planned schedule.identify the scheduled finish
actual finish
Time work actually ended, established through relevant evidence.verify the actual finish
employee recollection
Employee's remembered account of events or timing.record the employee recollection
work log
Record of work activity relevant to the review.review the work log
time exception
Missing or inconsistent timekeeping information requiring review.raise a time exception
supervisor review
Check carried out by the relevant supervising person.request supervisor review
internal cutoff
Organization's stated processing or approval deadline.meet the internal cutoff
approval status
Current state of the required permission or verification.report approval status
time correction
Authorized amendment to a time record based on the relevant evidence.document a time correction
source attribution
Identification of where a statement or figure came from.preserve source attribution
unverified entry
Record value that has not yet been established.avoid an unverified entry
exception queue
List of time or payroll issues awaiting review.maintain the exception queue
pay period
Defined interval covered by the relevant payroll.identify the pay period
timesheet approval
Confirmation of a time record under the actual process.distinguish timesheet approval
audit trail
Record showing the history and basis of changes.preserve the audit trail
scheduled hours
Work time indicated by the roster.separate scheduled hours
reported hours
Work time described by a person or source.qualify reported hours
verified hours
Work time established through the relevant review.distinguish verified hours
review outcome
Finding or unresolved status produced by a check.record the review outcome
payroll handoff
Transfer of relevant payroll information to the next responsible person.complete the payroll handoff

Hana can review the work log before the internal 14:00 cutoff. That deadline is for the fictional review process; it is not a rule about pay entitlement. Any correction needs its evidence, reviewer, and actual approval status preserved.

C Notice the language

Keep the evidence request separate from a judgment about the employee

A missing entry is a record problem, not proof of dishonesty or proof that no work occurred. Attribute the roster and recollection clearly. Ask the appropriate reviewer to examine the available log and report the verified status without forcing the record to fit a deadline.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the exception
Tuesday's timecard is missing a clock-out.
Name the planned time
The roster shows a 17:00 finish.
Attribute the recollection
Eli recalls finishing at 17:30.
State the unresolved fact
The actual finish time is not confirmed.
Request the evidence review
Could you review the work log?
Name the internal deadline
The approval cutoff is 14:00 today.
Avoid defaulting to the roster
I will not treat the scheduled finish as verified actual time.
Avoid defaulting to memory
The recollection needs to remain attributed.
Reject a false inference
A missing entry does not prove no work occurred.
Keep the request neutral
I am asking for verification, not assigning blame.
Preserve both sources
Please keep the roster and Eli's account visible.
Ask for the outcome
Report what the log supports and what remains unresolved.
Keep approval explicit
Has the time correction actually been approved?
Preserve the change history
The correction needs its supporting trail.
Avoid an invented entry
I will not fill the gap just to clear the queue.
Close with the handoff
The work-log review is pending; actual finish remains unconfirmed.
6 language and precision notes
Shows versus recalls
Distinguishes a planned schedule from an employee's remembered account.
Actual
Refers to what happened, not simply what was scheduled.
Before 14:00
Defines the internal review window, not a rule about wages.
Supports
Connects a conclusion to evidence.
Remain attributed
Keeps a reported claim attached to its source.
Just to clear
Identifies administrative pressure that does not establish a fact.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which exception note is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. Missing Tuesday clock-out; roster 17:00; Eli recalls 17:30; actual finish unconfirmed

The note preserves the missing entry, both attributed sources, and unresolved actual time.

2. What should Hana be asked to do?

Answer and explanation

B. Review the work log and report the supported finding

The supplied next step is evidence review by the supervisor.

3. Which statement about 14:00 is correct?

Answer and explanation

D. It is the fictional internal approval cutoff

The deadline concerns today's review process, not Tuesday's actual work time.

4. What should accompany an authorized correction?

Answer and explanation

D. The evidence and review trail

A traceable correction retains the basis and status of the change.

E Conversations

A missing clock-out is not a known finish time

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dev: Payroll assistant / Hana: Supervisor

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • audit trail
  • work log
  • scheduled finish
  • internal cutoff
  • review outcome
  • actual finish
  • approval status
  • payroll handoff
  • time exception
  • source attribution
  1. Dev
    Could you review Eli's missing Tuesday clock-out? The roster and recollection don't agree, and we need the evidence checked before today's internal cutoff.
  2. Hana
    Please give me the 1 for each time. I want to know what the roster says and what Eli reported before drawing any conclusion about the actual finish.
  3. Dev
    The roster says 17:00. Eli recalls 17:30, but the timecard has no end entry.
  4. Hana
    Then the 2 is still unconfirmed. We should not select seventeen hundred simply because it was scheduled, or seventeen thirty simply because it was recalled.
  5. Dev
    There's a work log available. Can you review it before 14:00 today?
  6. Hana
    Yes. I will review the 3 and report what it supports. Please keep the missing clock-out and both reported times visible while that review is pending.
  7. Dev
    I'm being asked to clear the queue. Entering the roster time now and checking later would make an assumption look verified.
  8. Hana
    Do not treat a 4 as verified actual time merely to clear the queue. The deadline does not create evidence that the missing entry currently lacks.
  9. Dev
    Please keep the request neutral. A missed clock-out doesn't mean Eli did no work.
  10. Hana
    Call it a 5 requiring review. We are checking a record gap; we have not established dishonesty, absence, or a confirmed finish time.
  11. Dev
    Once you begin checking, can I say 17:30 is approved, or do I need the actual result?
  12. Hana
    Wait for the 6. Starting a check is not approval of a particular time. If the evidence remains inconclusive, that unresolved status must be reported accurately.
  13. Dev
    And the 14:00 cutoff is today's internal review deadline, not a rule deciding Eli's entitlement to pay.
  14. Hana
    Correct. The 7 defines this processing window. It does not establish the actual hours worked or replace the applicable payroll and employment requirements.
  15. Dev
    When there's an authorized correction, the next colleague will need to see why the entry changed.
  16. Hana
    Preserve the 8. The supporting information and approval status should remain traceable through the actual workplace process, not be replaced by an unexplained number.
  17. Dev
    I'll hand over Tuesday clock-out missing, roster 17:00, Eli recalls 17:30, log review pending before 14:00.
  18. Hana
    That is an accurate 9. Add that actual finish remains unconfirmed, so the next person does not mistake the two candidate times for a completed decision.
  19. Dev
    I'll keep the exception visible and the actual finish unconfirmed until the review supplies a supported result.
  20. Hana
    Thank you. Keep the 10 explicit. I will review the available log and report the supported result or remaining uncertainty before the stated internal cutoff.

Answers and explanations
  1. source attribution Turn 2. Source attribution keeps the roster's 17:00 and Eli's recollection of 17:30 attached to their distinct origins.
  2. actual finish Turn 4. Actual finish is unconfirmed because the available schedule and recollection have not yet been verified against the work evidence.
  3. work log Turn 6. Work log is the available evidence Hana can examine before the internal approval cutoff.
  4. scheduled finish Turn 8. Scheduled finish is the roster's planned 17:00 time, not verified evidence of when Eli actually stopped work.
  5. time exception Turn 10. Time exception describes the missing or inconsistent record without accusing Eli or proving that no work occurred.
  6. review outcome Turn 12. Review outcome is the finding from examining the evidence, not the fact that Hana has started looking.
  7. internal cutoff Turn 14. Internal cutoff is the organization's fictional review deadline and does not determine employee pay rights.
  8. audit trail Turn 16. Audit trail records the evidence, review, and authorized change rather than hiding the original missing entry.
  9. payroll handoff Turn 18. Payroll handoff carries the record gap, source-specific times, pending evidence review, and internal deadline together.
  10. approval status Turn 20. Approval status remains unresolved until the relevant review and authorization establish any correction.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Dev: Could you review Eli's missing Tuesday clock-out? The roster and recollection don't agree, and we need the evidence checked before today's internal cutoff.
  2. Hana: Please give me the source attribution for each time. I want to know what the roster says and what Eli reported before drawing any conclusion about the actual finish.
  3. Dev: The roster says 17:00. Eli recalls 17:30, but the timecard has no end entry.
  4. Hana: Then the actual finish is still unconfirmed. We should not select seventeen hundred simply because it was scheduled, or seventeen thirty simply because it was recalled.
  5. Dev: There's a work log available. Can you review it before 14:00 today?
  6. Hana: Yes. I will review the work log and report what it supports. Please keep the missing clock-out and both reported times visible while that review is pending.
  7. Dev: I'm being asked to clear the queue. Entering the roster time now and checking later would make an assumption look verified.
  8. Hana: Do not treat a scheduled finish as verified actual time merely to clear the queue. The deadline does not create evidence that the missing entry currently lacks.
  9. Dev: Please keep the request neutral. A missed clock-out doesn't mean Eli did no work.
  10. Hana: Call it a time exception requiring review. We are checking a record gap; we have not established dishonesty, absence, or a confirmed finish time.
  11. Dev: Once you begin checking, can I say 17:30 is approved, or do I need the actual result?
  12. Hana: Wait for the review outcome. Starting a check is not approval of a particular time. If the evidence remains inconclusive, that unresolved status must be reported accurately.
  13. Dev: And the 14:00 cutoff is today's internal review deadline, not a rule deciding Eli's entitlement to pay.
  14. Hana: Correct. The internal cutoff defines this processing window. It does not establish the actual hours worked or replace the applicable payroll and employment requirements.
  15. Dev: When there's an authorized correction, the next colleague will need to see why the entry changed.
  16. Hana: Preserve the audit trail. The supporting information and approval status should remain traceable through the actual workplace process, not be replaced by an unexplained number.
  17. Dev: I'll hand over Tuesday clock-out missing, roster 17:00, Eli recalls 17:30, log review pending before 14:00.
  18. Hana: That is an accurate payroll handoff. Add that actual finish remains unconfirmed, so the next person does not mistake the two candidate times for a completed decision.
  19. Dev: I'll keep the exception visible and the actual finish unconfirmed until the review supplies a supported result.
  20. Hana: Thank you. Keep the approval status explicit. I will review the available log and report the supported result or remaining uncertainty before the stated internal cutoff.
Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Eli's missing Tuesday finish

A fictional payroll clerk requests evidence before the internal two o'clock cutoff.

  1. Payroll clerk: Eli's missing punch concerns Tuesday's clock-out. Could you check it before our two o'clock approval cutoff?
  2. Supervisor: The roster shows five. Would confirming that scheduled finish give you what you need?
  3. Payroll clerk: Not quite. Eli recalls five thirty, so we need the actual finish rather than the planned one.
  4. Supervisor: I can review the work log by one thirty. Does the exception show Tuesday's date?
  5. Payroll clerk: Yes, Tuesday the ninth. Please return either the supported correction or a clear unresolved status.
  6. Supervisor: Understood. If the log only repeats the roster, I can't treat it as independent confirmation.
  7. Payroll clerk: Exactly. We would still need someone who can confirm when the work actually ended.
  8. Supervisor: I'll speak to Eli if the log doesn't settle it and keep the reviewer informed.
  9. Payroll clerk: Thank you. The time exception remains open; no finish time has been confirmed during this call.
  10. Supervisor: I accept Tuesday's check and will return its status before two.
An overnight shift crosses the date line

A fictional payroll clerk and shift supervisor clarify two timecard entries.

  1. Payroll clerk: The timecard starts at ten on Thursday and ends at six on Friday. Is this one shift?
  2. Shift supervisor: Yes, it continued overnight. Why does the exception list Thursday as missing a finish?
  3. Payroll clerk: The import grouped entries by calendar date, so Friday's finish appears on a separate line.
  4. Shift supervisor: Then the person may have entered both punches correctly. Could you check the linked shift record?
  5. Payroll clerk: I have it open. The start and finish carry the same shift reference, N18.
  6. Shift supervisor: That supports treating them as linked records for review, rather than asking for an invented Thursday finish.
  7. Payroll clerk: Agreed. I will send both dated entries with N18 to the timekeeping reviewer.
  8. Shift supervisor: Please preserve the actual dates. We shouldn't make Friday's finish look as if it happened Thursday.
  9. Payroll clerk: I'll describe this as a date-grouping exception with both punches present.
  10. Shift supervisor: That's accurate. Any pay calculation can then use the reviewed record and the applicable rules.
Two supervisors each expect the other to approve

A fictional payroll assistant resolves ownership of an internal time approval.

  1. Payroll assistant: Dana's timecard is complete, but neither department has accepted the approval task for the transferred week.
  2. Team supervisor: I assumed the previous supervisor would approve it because Dana started the week there.
  3. Payroll assistant: They assumed you would approve the whole week. The internal cutoff is tomorrow at noon.
  4. Team supervisor: Can we clarify who reviews which dates? I supervised Thursday and Friday only.
  5. Payroll assistant: Yes. I need named reviewers for Monday through Wednesday and for Thursday through Friday.
  6. Team supervisor: I can accept Thursday and Friday. The earlier supervisor needs to confirm the first three days.
  7. Payroll assistant: I'll send that split to both of you and ask the earlier supervisor to acknowledge their part.
  8. Team supervisor: Please make clear that my acceptance is not confirmation of days I did not supervise.
  9. Payroll assistant: Understood. Your two-day review is assigned; the three-day review still needs an owner.
  10. Team supervisor: That gives us a concrete gap to resolve before noon rather than two assumptions of completion.

Lesson 04: Requesting evidence for time exceptions
Original fictional case: Employee Eli's Tuesday timecard has no clock-out entry. The roster shows a 17:00 finish, while Eli recalls finishing at 17:30. Supervisor Hana can review a work log before the internal approval cutoff at 14:00 today. Payroll assistant Dev must request that review and preserve both sources without choosing a finish time. Actual finish time is unconfirmed. The internal cutoff does not authorize an invented entry or determine Eli's pay rights.
Language workshop: A missing clock-out is not a known finish time
Communication goal: Request evidence for a time-record exception while distinguishing the roster, employee recollection, and confirmed actual work time.
Language guidance: The roster gives 17:00, Eli recalls 17:30, and the actual finish remains unconfirmed. Do not convert the scheduled time into verified work time or treat a missing clock-out as proof of no work.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Tuesday's timecard is missing a clock-out. / The roster shows a 17:00 finish. / Eli recalls finishing at 17:30. / The actual finish time is not confirmed. / Could you review the work log? / The approval cutoff is 14:00 today. / I will not treat the scheduled finish as verified actual time. / The recollection needs to remain attributed. / A missing entry does not prove no work occurred. / I am asking for verification, not assigning blame. / Please keep the roster and Eli's account visible. / Report what the log supports and what remains unresolved. / Has the time correction actually been approved? / The correction needs its supporting trail. / I will not fill the gap just to clear the queue. / The work-log review is pending; actual finish remains unconfirmed.
Vocabulary: timecard: Record of employee work-time entries. / clock-out: Entry recording the end of a work period. / clock-in: Entry recording the start of a work period. / roster: Planned schedule of assigned work. / scheduled finish: End time shown in the planned schedule. / actual finish: Time work actually ended, established through relevant evidence. / employee recollection: Employee's remembered account of events or timing. / work log: Record of work activity relevant to the review. / time exception: Missing or inconsistent timekeeping information requiring review. / supervisor review: Check carried out by the relevant supervising person. / internal cutoff: Organization's stated processing or approval deadline. / approval status: Current state of the required permission or verification. / time correction: Authorized amendment to a time record based on the relevant evidence. / source attribution: Identification of where a statement or figure came from. / unverified entry: Record value that has not yet been established. / exception queue: List of time or payroll issues awaiting review. / pay period: Defined interval covered by the relevant payroll. / timesheet approval: Confirmation of a time record under the actual process. / audit trail: Record showing the history and basis of changes. / scheduled hours: Work time indicated by the roster. / reported hours: Work time described by a person or source. / verified hours: Work time established through the relevant review. / review outcome: Finding or unresolved status produced by a check. / payroll handoff: Transfer of relevant payroll information to the next responsible person.
Speaking task: Complete the time-exception dialogue. Read the attributed times aloud: roster 17:00, Eli recalls 17:30, actual finish unconfirmed. Keep today's 14:00 internal review deadline separate. Complete the transfer and check the key. Repeat the evidence request without selecting an unsupported finish time.
Partner: Dev: Payroll assistant / Hana: Supervisor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the time-exception summary using the distinct sources and the actual review deadline.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Dev: I'll hand over Tuesday clock-out missing, roster 17:00, Eli recalls 17:30, log review pending before 14:00.
Hana: That is an accurate payroll handoff. Add that actual finish remains unconfirmed, so the next person does not mistake the two candidate times for a completed decision.
Dev: I'll keep the exception visible and the actual finish unconfirmed until the review supplies a supported result.
Hana: Thank you. Keep the approval status explicit. I will review the available log and report the supported result or remaining uncertainty before the stated internal cutoff.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Tuesday's timecard is missing a clock-out. / The roster shows a 17:00 finish. / Eli recalls finishing at 17:30. / The actual finish time is not confirmed. / Could you review the work log? / The approval cutoff is 14:00 today. / I will not treat the scheduled finish as verified actual time. / The recollection needs to remain attributed. / A missing entry does not prove no work occurred. / I am asking for verification, not assigning blame. / Please keep the roster and Eli's account visible. / Report what the log supports and what remains unresolved. / Has the time correction actually been approved? / The correction needs its supporting trail. / I will not fill the gap just to clear the queue. / The work-log review is pending; actual finish remains unconfirmed.
Vocabulary: timecard: Record of employee work-time entries. / clock-out: Entry recording the end of a work period. / clock-in: Entry recording the start of a work period. / roster: Planned schedule of assigned work. / scheduled finish: End time shown in the planned schedule. / actual finish: Time work actually ended, established through relevant evidence. / employee recollection: Employee's remembered account of events or timing. / work log: Record of work activity relevant to the review. / time exception: Missing or inconsistent timekeeping information requiring review. / supervisor review: Check carried out by the relevant supervising person. / internal cutoff: Organization's stated processing or approval deadline. / approval status: Current state of the required permission or verification. / time correction: Authorized amendment to a time record based on the relevant evidence. / source attribution: Identification of where a statement or figure came from. / unverified entry: Record value that has not yet been established. / exception queue: List of time or payroll issues awaiting review. / pay period: Defined interval covered by the relevant payroll. / timesheet approval: Confirmation of a time record under the actual process. / audit trail: Record showing the history and basis of changes. / scheduled hours: Work time indicated by the roster. / reported hours: Work time described by a person or source. / verified hours: Work time established through the relevant review. / review outcome: Finding or unresolved status produced by a check. / payroll handoff: Transfer of relevant payroll information to the next responsible person.
Speaking task: Complete the time-exception dialogue. Read the attributed times aloud: roster 17:00, Eli recalls 17:30, actual finish unconfirmed. Keep today's 14:00 internal review deadline separate. Complete the transfer and check the key. Repeat the evidence request without selecting an unsupported finish time.
Partner: Dev: Payroll assistant / Hana: Supervisor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the time-exception summary using the distinct sources and the actual review deadline.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Dev: I'll hand over Tuesday clock-out missing, roster 17:00, Eli recalls 17:30, log review pending before 14:00.
Hana: That is an accurate payroll handoff. Add that actual finish remains unconfirmed, so the next person does not mistake the two candidate times for a completed decision.
Dev: I'll keep the exception visible and the actual finish unconfirmed until the review supplies a supported result.
Hana: Thank you. Keep the approval status explicit. I will review the available log and report the supported result or remaining uncertainty before the stated internal cutoff.
END REFERENCE
05Explaining a payroll previewSame gross pay, fifty dollars less net+

Your goal

Explain the arithmetic of two payroll previews while separating a visible deduction from its unverified supporting authority.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

Same gross pay, fifty dollars less net

Preview A shows Morgan's gross pay of $1,200, deductions of $200, and net pay of $1,000. Preview B shows the same $1,200 gross pay, deductions of $250, and net pay of $950. A new $50 deduction line is present, but its supporting record is pending. Payroll assistant Leila must explain the numerical change to Morgan without calling it a salary cut, guessing the deduction's purpose, or declaring it authorized.

Morgan: Employee / Leila: Payroll assistant

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What stays the same in both previews?

Answer and explanation

B. Gross pay of $1,200

Both previews show gross pay of twelve hundred dollars.

2. Why is B's net pay $50 lower arithmetically?

Answer and explanation

A. Deductions increase from $200 to $250

The deduction total rises fifty dollars while gross pay stays unchanged.

3. What remains pending?

Answer and explanation

A. Support for the new $50 deduction

The supporting record for the new deduction line has not yet been supplied.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Both previews show $1,200 gross. A subtracts $200 to reach $1,000 net; B subtracts $250 to reach $950 net. The $50 net decrease corresponds to the $50 deduction increase, not a stated reduction in gross pay.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
payroll preview
Preliminary display of a payroll calculation before final processing.compare payroll previews
gross pay
Pay amount before the relevant deductions.state gross pay
deduction
Amount subtracted from pay in the stated calculation.identify a deduction
net pay
Amount remaining after the stated deductions.explain net pay
deduction total
Combined amount of the listed deductions.compare deduction totals
deduction line
Individual entry identifying an amount subtracted.query a new deduction line
supporting record
Evidence explaining or authorizing the relevant entry.request the supporting record
pay statement
Document showing the relevant earnings and deductions.read the pay statement
earnings line
Entry showing a component of pay before deductions.distinguish an earnings line
pay component
Separately identifiable part of remuneration or its calculation.identify the pay component
payroll register
Record summarizing payroll details for the relevant run.check the payroll register
pay run
Particular cycle of payroll calculation and processing.identify the pay run
preview comparison
Review of differences between preliminary calculations.prepare a preview comparison
gross-to-net
Relationship between gross pay, deductions, and the resulting net amount.explain gross-to-net movement
regular rate of pay
Overtime calculation rate including required pay components, not just base pay.verify the regular rate
nondiscretionary bonus
Bonus that does not meet the applicable discretionary-bonus exclusion criteria.review a nondiscretionary bonus
deduction authority
Valid basis or approval required for a deduction under applicable rules.verify deduction authority
payroll query
Request for clarification about a pay record or calculation.raise a payroll query
withholding
Amount retained from pay under the relevant applicable basis.distinguish withholding from gross pay
voluntary deduction
Deduction based on an applicable employee election or agreement.verify a voluntary deduction
statutory deduction
Deduction arising under relevant legal requirements.distinguish a statutory deduction
correction status
Whether a change has been requested, approved, or processed.report correction status
final payroll
Completed payroll at the relevant processing stage.distinguish a preview from final payroll
variance explanation
Account of how and why compared figures differ.give a variance explanation

A visible deduction line is not proof of its authority. The supporting record is pending, and the purpose has not been supplied. Do not label it tax, insurance, or a voluntary choice, and do not promise a refund or final pay outcome.

C Notice the language

Lead with the employee's actual question

A lower net figure can look like lower gross pay. Explain the unchanged gross amount and the increased deduction total plainly. Then address the missing support as a separate unresolved question; correct arithmetic does not prove a deduction is valid or approved.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the concern
You are asking why preview B shows less net pay.
Start with the unchanged figure
Gross pay remains $1,200 in both previews.
Explain preview A
A shows $200 in deductions and $1,000 net.
Explain preview B
B shows $250 in deductions and $950 net.
State the movement
Deductions rise by $50 and net pay falls by $50.
Identify the new line
Preview B includes a new $50 deduction.
Separate arithmetic and authority
The calculation explains the difference, not whether the line is authorized.
Name the evidence gap
The supporting record is still pending.
Avoid an invented purpose
I cannot identify this as tax or insurance from the information supplied.
Avoid a salary-cut claim
The previews do not show a reduction in gross pay.
Request the support
Please provide the record supporting the new deduction.
Keep the status provisional
We are discussing a preview, not a confirmed final payment.
Avoid a refund promise
No correction or refund has been established yet.
Keep personal details limited
We should use the appropriate private payroll channel.
Summarize precisely
Same gross pay; higher deductions; lower net pay; support pending.
Close with the unresolved question
The new line needs its basis verified before its status can be explained fully.
6 language and precision notes
Remains
Marks the gross-pay amount as unchanged.
Rise by and fall by
Describe opposite movements of the same fifty-dollar magnitude.
Includes
Establishes that a line appears, not that it is authorized.
Not whether
Separates a mathematical explanation from an approval question.
Still pending
Preserves the unresolved supporting-record status.
Preview, not final
Limits what can be claimed about a future payment.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which calculation matches preview B?

Answer and explanation

B. $1,200 minus $250 equals $950

Preview B retains the twelve-hundred-dollar gross and subtracts two hundred fifty.

2. Which explanation is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. Net falls because deductions rise, while gross remains unchanged

The supplied arithmetic establishes the movement but not the new line's purpose or authority.

3. Which reply addresses Morgan's unresolved question?

Answer and explanation

A. I will verify the new $50 line's purpose and authority from its supporting record

Morgan needs the basis for the new deduction, not only another subtraction; its purpose, authority, and any correction remain unestablished.

4. Which statement overclaims?

Answer and explanation

D. The new line is authorized because it appears in the preview

Appearance in a preliminary calculation does not establish the deduction's valid basis.

    A's 2 is $200, leaving $1,000 net. B subtracts $250 from the same gross, leaving $950.

    So the extra fifty deducted explains the fifty less left over. It isn't a fifty-dollar change to the twelve hundred.

    That's the 3 movement. The subtraction agrees, but we still need to check the basis of the new line.

Answers and explanations
  1. gross pay Turn 2. Gross pay remains twelve hundred dollars in both previews, so the supplied figures do not show a gross-pay cut.
  2. deduction total Turn 4. Deduction total is two hundred dollars in A and two hundred fifty in B.
  3. gross-to-net Turn 6. Gross-to-net explanation connects unchanged gross pay, increased deductions, and the resulting lower net amount.
  4. deduction line Turn 8. Deduction line is visible in preview B, but its purpose and supporting basis have not been supplied.
  5. payroll preview Turn 10. Payroll preview is a preliminary calculation and does not by itself prove approval of every line.
  6. payroll query Turn 12. Payroll query concerns the basis of the new fifty-dollar deduction, not the subtraction itself.
  7. supporting record Turn 14. Supporting record is the missing evidence needed to explain the new deduction's basis and status.
  8. final payroll Turn 16. Final payroll has not been established by comparing these preliminary records with unresolved supporting documentation.
  9. correction status Turn 18. Correction status is not yet a completed change or refund; the current action is a private supporting-record query.
  10. variance explanation Turn 20. Variance explanation states the numerical movement while retaining the unresolved basis of the new deduction.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Morgan's fifty-dollar preview difference

A fictional payroll reviewer and clerk compare previews A and B for the same employee and period.

  1. Reviewer: Preview B shows nine hundred fifty net pay. Did Morgan's gross pay fall by fifty?
  2. Payroll clerk: No. Gross pay stays at twelve hundred. Deductions rise from two hundred to two hundred fifty.
  3. Reviewer: So the extra deduction explains the displayed net difference, but not necessarily whether that deduction belongs?
  4. Payroll clerk: Exactly. The new fifty-dollar line has no supporting record attached yet, so its basis remains unconfirmed.
  5. Reviewer: Are we comparing the same employee and period, rather than differently filtered reports?
  6. Payroll clerk: Yes, Morgan and the same period. I checked both headings before comparing the lines.
  7. Reviewer: Please don't label the additional deduction as a tax change without evidence of its actual category.
  8. Payroll clerk: I won't. I'll refer the new line and missing support to the payroll lead.
  9. Reviewer: Then the preview comparison is complete, but approval of that line remains a separate question.
  10. Payroll clerk: Correct. Gross unchanged, deductions fifty higher, net fifty lower, with the new line awaiting review.
A one-time amount disappears from the next preview

A fictional employee asks about differing gross figures across two payroll previews.

  1. Employee: My current preview is lower than the previous one. Has someone changed my regular rate?
  2. Payroll clerk: The earlier preview includes a separate one-time payment. The regular earnings lines show the same rate.
  3. Employee: Which line accounts for the difference? I only compared the total at the bottom.
  4. Payroll clerk: The line labeled recognition payment appears in the earlier period for one hundred dollars only.
  5. Employee: So that explains the gross difference between these two previews, without implying a lower regular rate?
  6. Payroll clerk: Yes. The displayed regular rate is unchanged. I can show you the two lines separately.
  7. Employee: I also have a question about a deduction, but that sounds like a different comparison.
  8. Payroll clerk: It is. Let's identify the deduction reference and have the payroll specialist review its basis.
  9. Employee: Thank you. For the earnings question, I'll compare regular amounts separately from the one-time payment.
  10. Payroll clerk: That gives a clearer comparison. The separate deduction question remains open for the specialist.
One more employee in the register

A fictional payroll assistant explains a headcount difference to a reviewer.

  1. Reviewer: The payroll register lists twenty-one employees, but the department list has twenty. Which one is wrong?
  2. Payroll assistant: The department list excludes transferred staff. The register includes one employee with earnings for the earlier dates.
  3. Reviewer: So the two totals may use different populations rather than reflect a duplicate employee?
  4. Payroll assistant: Yes. The extra name is Rowan, whose transfer took effect halfway through the pay period.
  5. Reviewer: Have you confirmed that Rowan appears only once in the register we're reviewing now?
  6. Payroll assistant: Yes, once under the same employee reference. The earnings allocation still needs the department split checked.
  7. Reviewer: Then let's not remove the line simply to make the headcount agree with the current list.
  8. Payroll assistant: Agreed. I'll explain the population difference and show the transfer date beside Rowan's record.
  9. Reviewer: Please identify the remaining department-allocation review separately from the already-checked duplicate-name question.
  10. Payroll assistant: I have. Twenty-one register entries, twenty current department members, and one transfer allocation awaiting review.

Numerical explanations use only the stated facts.
Lesson 05: Explaining a payroll preview
Original fictional case: Preview A shows Morgan's gross pay of $1,200, deductions of $200, and net pay of $1,000. Preview B shows the same $1,200 gross pay, deductions of $250, and net pay of $950. A new $50 deduction line is present, but its supporting record is pending. Payroll assistant Leila must explain the numerical change to Morgan without calling it a salary cut, guessing the deduction's purpose, or declaring it authorized.
Language workshop: Same gross pay, fifty dollars less net
Communication goal: Explain the arithmetic of two payroll previews while separating a visible deduction from its unverified supporting authority.
Language guidance: Both previews show $1,200 gross. A subtracts $200 to reach $1,000 net; B subtracts $250 to reach $950 net. The $50 net decrease corresponds to the $50 deduction increase, not a stated reduction in gross pay.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): You are asking why preview B shows less net pay. / Gross pay remains $1,200 in both previews. / A shows $200 in deductions and $1,000 net. / B shows $250 in deductions and $950 net. / Deductions rise by $50 and net pay falls by $50. / Preview B includes a new $50 deduction. / The calculation explains the difference, not whether the line is authorized. / The supporting record is still pending. / I cannot identify this as tax or insurance from the information supplied. / The previews do not show a reduction in gross pay. / Please provide the record supporting the new deduction. / We are discussing a preview, not a confirmed final payment. / No correction or refund has been established yet. / We should use the appropriate private payroll channel. / Same gross pay; higher deductions; lower net pay; support pending. / The new line needs its basis verified before its status can be explained fully.
Vocabulary: payroll preview: Preliminary display of a payroll calculation before final processing. / gross pay: Pay amount before the relevant deductions. / deduction: Amount subtracted from pay in the stated calculation. / net pay: Amount remaining after the stated deductions. / deduction total: Combined amount of the listed deductions. / deduction line: Individual entry identifying an amount subtracted. / supporting record: Evidence explaining or authorizing the relevant entry. / pay statement: Document showing the relevant earnings and deductions. / earnings line: Entry showing a component of pay before deductions. / pay component: Separately identifiable part of remuneration or its calculation. / payroll register: Record summarizing payroll details for the relevant run. / pay run: Particular cycle of payroll calculation and processing. / preview comparison: Review of differences between preliminary calculations. / gross-to-net: Relationship between gross pay, deductions, and the resulting net amount. / regular rate of pay: Overtime calculation rate including required pay components, not just base pay. / nondiscretionary bonus: Bonus that does not meet the applicable discretionary-bonus exclusion criteria. / deduction authority: Valid basis or approval required for a deduction under applicable rules. / payroll query: Request for clarification about a pay record or calculation. / withholding: Amount retained from pay under the relevant applicable basis. / voluntary deduction: Deduction based on an applicable employee election or agreement. / statutory deduction: Deduction arising under relevant legal requirements. / correction status: Whether a change has been requested, approved, or processed. / final payroll: Completed payroll at the relevant processing stage. / variance explanation: Account of how and why compared figures differ.
Speaking task: Complete the payroll-preview dialogue. Read A and B in the same order: gross, deductions, net. Then read Morgan's question about the new $50 line and Leila's evidence request. Complete the transfer and check the key. Keep correct subtraction separate from authorization and final payment.
Partner: Morgan: Employee / Leila: Payroll assistant
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the gross-to-net readback. Preserve the unchanged gross figure and the pending support.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Morgan: Please handle my records through the appropriate payroll contact. I don't want this sent to people who aren't reviewing it.
Leila: Of course. I'll report the correction status through the appropriate private process. Currently support is pending, with no completed change established.
Morgan: Then the summary is same gross, fifty more deducted, fifty less net, and the reason for that new deduction still needs checking.
Leila: Yes. That's the variance explanation the records support. I'll carry the specific evidence question forward without treating the preview as final approval.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level.
