06Making approval status unambiguousSeven approved items, no released batch+

Your goal

Distinguish invoice approval, proposed batch membership, payment release, and an excluded exception in a concise status handoff.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Seven approved items, no released batch

A fictional payment set contains eight invoices. Seven have recorded approval. Invoice V22 awaits a receiving document and is excluded from the proposed seven-item batch. The batch itself has not been released, and the approver returns at 15:00. Payment preparer Ben briefs colleague Imani, who must not tell suppliers that all eight invoices are approved or that any payment has already been sent. Return time is not a guaranteed release time.

Imani: Finance colleague / Ben: Payment preparer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How many invoices have recorded approval?

Answer and explanation

C. Seven

Seven of the eight invoices have recorded approval in the supplied payment set.

2. Why is V22 excluded from the proposal?

Answer and explanation

D. Its receiving document is missing

V22 awaits receiving evidence and is not part of the proposed seven-item batch.

3. What does 15:00 establish?

Answer and explanation

D. The approver's return time

The stated time concerns availability of the approver, not a completed payment decision.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

There are eight invoices in the original set, seven with recorded approval, and seven in the proposed batch. V22 is excluded pending its receiving document. Do not describe V22 as approved simply because the other seven are approved.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
payment batch
Group of payment items prepared for joint processing.prepare a payment batch
batch proposal
Suggested set of items for the next payment process.distinguish the batch proposal
invoice approval
Recorded permission for the specified invoice under the relevant process.verify invoice approval
recorded approval
Permission evidenced in the available record.confirm recorded approval
batch release
Authorized step sending the prepared batch into the relevant payment process.distinguish batch release
payment execution
Carrying out the payment instruction at the relevant stage.verify payment execution
payment confirmation
Evidence that the specified payment action occurred.request payment confirmation
excluded item
Entry deliberately left out of the proposed set.identify the excluded item
receiving evidence
Record supporting receipt of the relevant goods.obtain receiving evidence
approval dependency
Missing condition required before the next approval stage.state the approval dependency
approver
Person authorized to give the relevant permission.contact the approver
return time
Time when a person is expected to be available again.state the return time
authorization limit
Boundary of what a recorded approval permits.preserve the authorization limit
batch membership
Items included in the specified group.confirm batch membership
control stage
Distinct step in checking or authorizing a transaction.identify the control stage
release status
Whether the relevant batch has actually been released.report release status
payment status
Current stage of the payment process.clarify payment status
exception item
Entry requiring separate attention before the process can proceed.track the exception item
pending evidence
Supporting material not yet available.identify pending evidence
supplier update
Communication to a vendor about the relevant account position.qualify a supplier update
remittance notice
Message describing a payment or its intended invoice application.verify a remittance notice
approval trail
Record of who approved what and at which stage.retain the approval trail
batch count
Number of entries in a specified payment group.reconcile the batch count
status readback
Restatement of the stages and unresolved conditions.give a status readback

The seven-item batch has not been released. The approver returns at 15:00, but that is not a promise of release or supplier receipt of funds. Keep invoice approval, proposed inclusion, release, and payment confirmation separate.

C Notice the language

Use separate verbs for separate control stages

Approved, included, released, and paid are not interchangeable. A brief update should identify the approved items, the proposed batch, and any excluded exception. Naming the approver's return helps plan follow-up, but it does not promise the outcome of that review.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the original set
We began with eight invoices.
State the approved count
Seven have recorded approval.
Name the exception
V22 is awaiting its receiving document.
State the exclusion
V22 is excluded from the proposed batch.
Confirm the proposed count
The proposal contains seven items.
Keep item and batch stages separate
Invoice approval is not the same as batch release.
State release status
The batch has not been released.
Avoid a payment claim
We have no confirmation that payment has been sent.
Name the availability
The approver returns at 15:00.
Limit the timing inference
That return time does not guarantee release.
Preserve the missing evidence
V22 still needs receiving support.
Correct an overbroad update
It would be inaccurate to say all eight are approved.
Request a stage-specific answer
Are you asking about approval, inclusion, or release?
Keep suppliers informed accurately
I can report the current stage without promising payment.
Read back the whole position
Seven approved, V22 excluded, seven proposed, none confirmed released.
Close with the next review
We will seek the relevant review when the approver is available.
6 language and precision notes
Have recorded approval
Limits the claim to permission evidenced for the specified items.
Excluded from
Defines batch membership without canceling the invoice.
Has not been released
States the actual batch stage.
Returns at
Describes availability, not an approval outcome.
All eight
Identifies the overgeneralization that the exception makes inaccurate.
Are you asking about ...?
Clarifies which control stage the other person means.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which status line is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. Seven invoices approved; V22 excluded; proposed batch unreleased

The correct line preserves item approval, exclusion, proposal, and release status.

2. What would overstate the meaning of 15:00?

Answer and explanation

C. Payments are guaranteed to be sent at 15:00

Return time does not guarantee an approval or payment-release outcome.

3. Which count belongs to the proposed batch?

Answer and explanation

A. Seven

The proposal excludes V22 and therefore contains the seven other invoices.

4. What information is still needed for V22?

Answer and explanation

A. The receiving document

V22 is explicitly awaiting evidence of receipt before the exception can be resolved.

E Conversations

Seven approved items, no released batch

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Imani: Finance colleague / Ben: Payment preparer

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • excluded item
  • recorded approval
  • batch membership
  • approval trail
  • exception item
  • supplier update
  • receiving evidence
  • control stage
  • return time
  • release status
  1. Imani
    A supplier asked whether the run is approved. There were eight invoices originally; are all eight ready to go?
  2. Ben
    No. Seven have 1. Invoice V22 is still awaiting a receiving document, and it is excluded from the proposed batch.
  3. Imani
    Then the proposed batch has seven, not eight. V22 stays outside it.
  4. Ben
    Correct. The 2 has changed. Keep V22 visible as an exception, but do not count it among the seven proposed payment items.
  5. Imani
    Have those seven been released, or are we only talking about invoice approval?
  6. Ben
    The 3 is not released. Approval of the seven invoices does not mean the batch itself has passed the release stage or that payment has been sent.
  7. Imani
    What receiving document is missing for V22? I need a reason, not just held.
  8. Ben
    It is awaiting 4, specifically the receiving document. That requirement remains open; the invoice has not become approved because the other seven are.
  9. Imani
    The approver is back at three. Is that a release time I can give the supplier?
  10. Ben
    It is the 5, fifteen hundred. It tells us when the approver returns, not what the review outcome will be or when any payment will reach a supplier.
  11. Imani
    I'll keep 15:00 as the approver's return, with no promised release time.
  12. Ben
    Exactly. Preserve the 6. A useful update names where the process stands instead of using approved as a shorthand for every later step.
  13. Imani
    Could you read back the status for the team, so we don't use approved to mean several different things?
  14. Ben
    Seven invoices have approval; V22 is an 7 pending its receiving document. The seven-item proposal is not released. The approver returns at fifteen hundred, with no release time guaranteed.
  15. Imani
    Does excluded mean canceled? I don't want someone telling V22's supplier the invoice has been rejected permanently.
  16. Ben
    No. It remains an 8 needing its supporting document. Its absence from this proposal is not a cancellation decision or a statement that the supplier has no valid invoice.
  17. Imani
    I'll retain seven approved, seven proposed, V22 excluded, and the batch unreleased.
  18. Ben
    Yes. The 9 should show the actual permissions while the release status remains separate. Nobody should infer payment confirmation merely from the existence of item approvals.
  19. Imani
    The supplier update will name the stage we actually know, without saying the money has gone.
  20. Ben
    Good. A 10 can be specific without overpromising. The next review and any later release must be confirmed through the actual process before we report them as complete.

Answers and explanations
  1. recorded approval Turn 2. Recorded approval applies to seven invoices, not all eight in the original payment set.
  2. batch membership Turn 4. Batch membership is seven items because V22 has been excluded from the proposal.
  3. release status Turn 6. Release status remains unreleased even though the seven included invoices have recorded approval.
  4. receiving evidence Turn 8. Receiving evidence is the missing document for V22 and explains its exclusion from the proposed batch.
  5. return time Turn 10. Return time is 15:00 for the approver, not a guaranteed batch-release or payment time.
  6. control stage Turn 12. Control stage distinguishes the unreleased proposal from invoice approval and later payment execution.
  7. excluded item Turn 14. Excluded item identifies V22 as outside the proposed seven-item batch while its receiving document is pending.
  8. exception item Turn 16. Exception item keeps V22 active for follow-up without treating exclusion as cancellation or permanent rejection.
  9. approval trail Turn 18. Approval trail records the actual permissions without substituting them for uncompleted release or payment stages.
  10. supplier update Turn 20. Supplier update should report the supported process stage without promising that payment has been sent or will arrive at a set time.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Imani: A supplier asked whether the run is approved. There were eight invoices originally; are all eight ready to go?
  2. Ben: No. Seven have recorded approval. Invoice V22 is still awaiting a receiving document, and it is excluded from the proposed batch.
  3. Imani: Then the proposed batch has seven, not eight. V22 stays outside it.
  4. Ben: Correct. The batch membership has changed. Keep V22 visible as an exception, but do not count it among the seven proposed payment items.
  5. Imani: Have those seven been released, or are we only talking about invoice approval?
  6. Ben: The release status is not released. Approval of the seven invoices does not mean the batch itself has passed the release stage or that payment has been sent.
  7. Imani: What receiving document is missing for V22? I need a reason, not just held.
  8. Ben: It is awaiting receiving evidence, specifically the receiving document. That requirement remains open; the invoice has not become approved because the other seven are.
  9. Imani: The approver is back at three. Is that a release time I can give the supplier?
  10. Ben: It is the return time, fifteen hundred. It tells us when the approver returns, not what the review outcome will be or when any payment will reach a supplier.
  11. Imani: I'll keep 15:00 as the approver's return, with no promised release time.
  12. Ben: Exactly. Preserve the control stage. A useful update names where the process stands instead of using approved as a shorthand for every later step.
  13. Imani: Could you read back the status for the team, so we don't use approved to mean several different things?
  14. Ben: Seven invoices have approval; V22 is an excluded item pending its receiving document. The seven-item proposal is not released. The approver returns at fifteen hundred, with no release time guaranteed.
  15. Imani: Does excluded mean canceled? I don't want someone telling V22's supplier the invoice has been rejected permanently.
  16. Ben: No. It remains an exception item needing its supporting document. Its absence from this proposal is not a cancellation decision or a statement that the supplier has no valid invoice.

  19. Imani: The supplier update will name the stage we actually know, without saying the money has gone.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Seven approved invoices are not seven completed payments

A fictional clerk updates a manager on the proposed batch and excluded V22.

  1. Manager: Can I tell purchasing that the eight-invoice payment batch has been paid this morning?
  2. Accounts clerk: No. Seven invoices have recorded approval, but their proposed batch hasn't been released. V22 remains excluded.
  3. Manager: What's missing for V22, and does its problem affect the description of the other seven?
  4. Accounts clerk: Its receiving document is missing. The other seven have approval, though none is reported as released here.
  5. Manager: When will someone review the proposed batch rather than simply the individual invoice records?
  6. Accounts clerk: The approver returns at three. I can present the seven-item proposal then.
  7. Manager: Please make the status clear now, because purchasing is waiting to answer a supplier's question.
  8. Accounts clerk: Seven invoices approved, proposed batch not released, V22 excluded pending receipt evidence. That's the current position.
  9. Manager: Thank you. I'll relay those separate stages and avoid promising a payment date to the supplier.
  10. Accounts clerk: I'll update the release status when confirmed and keep the V22 document query visible separately.
A delegation that covers only smaller invoices

A fictional clerk checks a substitute reviewer's stated authority.

  1. Accounts clerk: The usual approver is away. Could you confirm whether your delegation covers this twelve-hundred-dollar invoice?
  2. Deputy manager: I can approve department purchases up to one thousand. I hadn't noticed this one exceeded that limit.
  3. Accounts clerk: Thanks for checking. The approval matrix also names the finance manager for items above that threshold.
  4. Deputy manager: Then forwarding it to me wouldn't complete approval, even though I manage the department today.
  5. Accounts clerk: Correct. We need the named finance review, not just a senior colleague's acknowledgment.
  6. Deputy manager: I can confirm that the goods were received, but I can't supply that final approval.
  7. Accounts clerk: That confirmation is useful support. I'll keep it separate from the remaining approval requirement.
  8. Deputy manager: Please tell the finance manager what I've verified so they don't have to ask again.
  9. Accounts clerk: I'll include your receipt confirmation and explain that your delegated approver limit is exceeded.
  10. Deputy manager: Then the handover is clear: receipt checked, finance approval pending, and no payment release confirmed.
The attachment is not the approved version

A fictional invoice clerk checks conflicting attachment versions with an approver.

  1. Invoice clerk: Your approval note refers to version two, but the batch attachment is still version one.
  2. Approver: Version two removes a delivery charge. Can you see that difference in the attached amount?
  3. Invoice clerk: Yes. The batch shows the higher amount from version one, so the approval and attachment don't agree.
  4. Approver: Please present the corrected attachment for review before anyone describes the batch total as approved.
  5. Invoice clerk: Should I retain version one in the history with a clear superseded label?
  6. Approver: Yes, that explains why the amount changed. The current proposal should point clearly to version two.
  7. Invoice clerk: I've corrected the proposal reference and recalculated its displayed total for your review.
  8. Approver: I can now see version two and the reduced amount. That resolves the document-version mismatch.
  9. Invoice clerk: I'll record your review of this proposal separately from any later payment release.
  10. Approver: Please do. We have clarified the approved amount here, not confirmed that money has left the bank.

Released names the separate stage that the proposed batch has not completed.

Lesson 06: Making approval status unambiguous
Original fictional case: A fictional payment set contains eight invoices. Seven have recorded approval. Invoice V22 awaits a receiving document and is excluded from the proposed seven-item batch. The batch itself has not been released, and the approver returns at 15:00. Payment preparer Ben briefs colleague Imani, who must not tell suppliers that all eight invoices are approved or that any payment has already been sent. Return time is not a guaranteed release time.
Language workshop: Seven approved items, no released batch
Communication goal: Distinguish invoice approval, proposed batch membership, payment release, and an excluded exception in a concise status handoff.
Language guidance: There are eight invoices in the original set, seven with recorded approval, and seven in the proposed batch. V22 is excluded pending its receiving document. Do not describe V22 as approved simply because the other seven are approved.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): We began with eight invoices. / Seven have recorded approval. / V22 is awaiting its receiving document. / V22 is excluded from the proposed batch. / The proposal contains seven items. / Invoice approval is not the same as batch release. / The batch has not been released. / We have no confirmation that payment has been sent. / The approver returns at 15:00. / That return time does not guarantee release. / V22 still needs receiving support. / It would be inaccurate to say all eight are approved. / Are you asking about approval, inclusion, or release? / I can report the current stage without promising payment. / Seven approved, V22 excluded, seven proposed, none confirmed released. / We will seek the relevant review when the approver is available.
Vocabulary: payment batch: Group of payment items prepared for joint processing. / batch proposal: Suggested set of items for the next payment process. / invoice approval: Recorded permission for the specified invoice under the relevant process. / recorded approval: Permission evidenced in the available record. / batch release: Authorized step sending the prepared batch into the relevant payment process. / payment execution: Carrying out the payment instruction at the relevant stage. / payment confirmation: Evidence that the specified payment action occurred. / excluded item: Entry deliberately left out of the proposed set. / receiving evidence: Record supporting receipt of the relevant goods. / approval dependency: Missing condition required before the next approval stage. / approver: Person authorized to give the relevant permission. / return time: Time when a person is expected to be available again. / authorization limit: Boundary of what a recorded approval permits. / batch membership: Items included in the specified group. / control stage: Distinct step in checking or authorizing a transaction. / release status: Whether the relevant batch has actually been released. / payment status: Current stage of the payment process. / exception item: Entry requiring separate attention before the process can proceed. / pending evidence: Supporting material not yet available. / supplier update: Communication to a vendor about the relevant account position. / remittance notice: Message describing a payment or its intended invoice application. / approval trail: Record of who approved what and at which stage. / batch count: Number of entries in a specified payment group. / status readback: Restatement of the stages and unresolved conditions.
Speaking task: Complete the payment-batch dialogue. Read the full status aloud: eight original invoices, seven approved, V22 excluded, seven proposed, batch unreleased, approver back at 15:00. Complete the transfer and check the key. Repeat the supplier update without promising release or receipt of funds.
Partner: Imani: Finance colleague / Ben: Payment preparer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the payment-status handoff without turning approval into payment confirmation.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Imani: I'll retain seven approved, seven proposed, V22 excluded, and the batch unreleased.
Ben: Yes. The approval trail should show the actual permissions while the release status remains separate. Nobody should infer payment confirmation merely from the existence of item approvals.
Imani: The supplier update will name the stage we actually know, without saying the money has gone.
Ben: Good. A supplier update can be specific without overpromising. The next review and any later release must be confirmed through the actual process before we report them as complete.
END REFERENCE

3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Handing over a period-close reviewNine checks done, one period question open+

Your goal

Hand over an unresolved cutoff question with its amount, dates, review owner, and unapproved posting status intact.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Nine checks done, one period question open

The fictional June close has nine of ten checklist items complete. A $240 maintenance invoice is dated July 2 but describes June service. The accountant must review its posting period; no adjusting entry has been approved. Alex takes over the open-item follow-up from Hana at noon. The handoff must distinguish document date from service period and follow-up responsibility from authority to choose or post an accounting entry.

Hana: Outgoing bookkeeper / Alex: Incoming bookkeeper

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How many checklist items are complete?

Answer and explanation

B. Nine of ten

Nine items are complete, with one period-close review still outstanding.

2. What dates differ on the maintenance item?

Answer and explanation

B. Invoice dated July 2; service described as June

The document date and stated service period fall in different months.

3. What does Alex take over at noon?

Answer and explanation

A. Follow-up on the open item

Alex takes over follow-up while the accountant's period review and entry approval remain unresolved.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The invoice amount is $240, its date is July 2, and its description refers to June service. Those are distinct facts. The accountant must review the posting period; the assistant should not choose July solely from the invoice date.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
period close
Process of completing accounting work for a defined reporting interval.hand over the period close
close checklist
List of required tasks for finishing the reporting period.update the close checklist
open item
Matter still unresolved or incomplete.retain the open item
invoice date
Date printed or assigned to the supplier document.identify the invoice date
service period
Interval during which the invoiced service was provided.distinguish the service period
posting period
Accounting period to which an entry is assigned.request posting-period review
cutoff question
Issue about which period should contain a transaction or amount.raise a cutoff question
maintenance expense
Cost associated with relevant maintenance work.identify the maintenance expense
accountant review
Evaluation by the relevant accounting professional.obtain accountant review
adjusting entry
Accounting entry addressing the relevant period-end recognition or measurement.distinguish an adjusting entry
accrual
Recognition of an amount in the relevant period before the associated cash settlement.discuss an accrual question
accrued expense
Expense recognized with a related obligation before payment.identify an accrued-expense review
prepayment
Payment made before the related benefit or service is fully received.distinguish a prepayment
supporting invoice
Supplier bill retained as evidence for the review.attach the supporting invoice
posting approval
Permission for the specified accounting entry.verify posting approval
unposted item
Record or proposed transaction not yet entered in the ledger.identify an unposted item
journal entry
Record of a transaction using the relevant accounting accounts.review the journal entry
general ledger
Main accounting record containing account balances and entries.distinguish the general ledger
follow-up owner
Person responsible for pursuing the next response or action.name the follow-up owner
handover time
Time responsibility passes to the next person.confirm the handover time
close status
Current completion stage of the period-end process.report close status
carrying amount
Amount at which an asset or liability is recorded after relevant recognized adjustments.reconcile the carrying amount
recognition schedule
Record allocating an amount to accounting periods under the specified treatment.review the recognition schedule
cutoff evidence
Dates and service information relevant to the period question.retain cutoff evidence

Nine of ten checklist items are complete, so the close is not fully finished. Alex takes follow-up ownership at noon, but no adjusting entry has been approved. Follow-up responsibility does not establish authority to decide or post the entry.

C Notice the language

Transfer responsibility without pretending the decision transferred too

A good handoff gives the next person the unresolved question and the evidence needed to pursue it. It does not turn a follow-up owner into an accounting approver. Keep the incomplete checklist item visible even when the other nine tasks are finished.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the close position
Nine of ten checklist items are complete.
Name the open item
The maintenance invoice still needs period review.
Give the amount
The invoice is for $240.
Give the document date
It is dated July 2.
Give the service period
The description refers to June service.
Identify the distinction
The invoice date and service period are not the same fact.
Name the reviewer
The accountant needs to review the posting period.
Preserve approval status
No adjusting entry has been approved.
Avoid an automatic period choice
I will not assign July merely from the invoice date.
Transfer the follow-up
Alex takes over the open-item follow-up at noon.
Separate roles
Owning the follow-up does not mean approving the entry.
Keep the evidence together
Retain the invoice and its June-service description.
Avoid false completion
The close is not ten out of ten yet.
State the dependency
This item remains open pending the accountant's review.
Request a clear result
Please record the reviewed period and actual approval status.
Close with the handoff
Nine complete, one open, Alex following up, no entry approved.
6 language and precision notes
Dated versus describes
Distinguishes the document's date from the service period it reports.
Still needs
Marks the review as outstanding.
Merely from
Identifies an insufficient basis for a decision.
Takes over at noon
Specifies when follow-up responsibility changes.
Does not mean approving
Separates administrative ownership from accounting authority.
Nine complete, one open
Prevents a high completion count from becoming a claim of full closure.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which handoff preserves the facts?

Answer and explanation

D. $240 invoice dated July 2 for June service; accountant review pending

The summary separates amount, document date, service period, and unresolved review.

2. What does nine of ten complete mean?

Answer and explanation

C. One checklist item remains open

The completion count leaves one required checklist item unfinished.

3. Which responsibility changes at noon?

Answer and explanation

A. Alex takes over follow-up

The brief assigns follow-up ownership rather than authority for an accounting decision.

4. Which statement is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

B. The adjusting entry has already been approved

No adjusting entry has been approved in the supplied close status.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

Nine checks done, one period question open

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Hana: Outgoing bookkeeper / Alex: Incoming bookkeeper

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • adjusting entry
  • handover time
  • cutoff evidence
  • close status
  • posting approval
  • open item
  • accountant review
  • follow-up owner
  • invoice date
  • cutoff question
  1. Hana
    Alex, you're taking over at noon. Nine June-close checks are complete; the maintenance invoice is the one still awaiting review.
  2. Alex
    Please give me the 1 first, then the invoice details and the decision we are waiting for. I do not want the one open item hidden behind the completed count.
  3. Hana
    It's $240, dated July 2, but the description says June service.
  4. Alex
    Then we need to distinguish the 2 from the service period. The printed July date should not make us overlook the fact that the description relates to June.
  5. Hana
    The accountant needs to decide the posting period. I haven't treated the July date as an automatic answer.
  6. Alex
    I will retain that 3 as unresolved. The amount, document date, and service description give the reviewer the facts without our substituting an assumed answer.
  7. Hana
    There isn't an approved adjusting entry either. Knowing the amount doesn't mean the entry is ready.
  8. Alex
    I will make the 4 status explicit. Knowing the amount does not establish that the period treatment or any proposed entry has been approved.
  9. Hana
    At noon you're taking over the follow-up, not the accountant's authority to approve the treatment.
  10. Alex
    Understood. I become the 5, not the accounting approver. I will pursue the response and preserve the decision with its actual reviewer and approval status.
  11. Hana
    Please retain June service beside July 2. A note saying only July invoice would lose the issue.
  12. Alex
    I will retain the 6 together: two hundred forty dollars, July second invoice date, June service. None of those details should disappear in the summary.
  13. Hana
    Leave one item open after the handoff. Changing its owner doesn't complete it.
  14. Alex
    Correct. The 7 stays visible. A change of owner is not a completed review, and nine out of ten must not become ten out of ten just because the handoff is done.
  15. Hana
    When the accountant responds, distinguish a period decision, an entry approval, and a request for more evidence.
  16. Alex
    I will preserve those distinctions in the 8 result. A request for more evidence is not approval, and a period discussion should not be silently expanded into permission to post.
  17. Hana
    For now, no adjustment is authorized by this handoff. We just need the actual question and support reviewed.
  18. Alex
    Agreed. An 9 has not been approved, so the handoff should not describe one as ready or already recorded. I will keep that limit attached to the item.
  19. Hana
    Readback: nine complete, one open; $240 July 2 invoice for June service; accountant review pending; you follow up from noon.
  20. Alex
    That is accurate. The 10 changes who pursues the response, not the unresolved accounting status. I will take the follow-up with all those facts intact.

Answers and explanations
  1. close status Turn 2. Close status is nine of ten checklist items complete, not a fully finished June close.
  2. invoice date Turn 4. Invoice date is July 2, separate from the June service period described on the document.
  3. cutoff question Turn 6. Cutoff question concerns the appropriate posting period for a July-dated invoice describing June service.
  4. posting approval Turn 8. Posting approval has not been given for an adjusting entry, even though the invoice amount is known.
  5. follow-up owner Turn 10. Follow-up owner is Alex from noon, responsible for pursuing the review rather than approving the accounting entry.
  6. cutoff evidence Turn 12. Cutoff evidence includes the July 2 document date and June service description needed for the accountant's review.
  7. open item Turn 14. Open item remains incomplete after ownership transfers because the accountant's review is still pending.
  8. accountant review Turn 16. Accountant review must be reported according to its actual outcome, not assumed to include posting approval.
  9. adjusting entry Turn 18. Adjusting entry is a possible accounting action requiring the relevant review and approval, not something authorized by this handoff.
  10. handover time Turn 20. Handover time is noon, when follow-up responsibility passes to Alex without completing or approving the open item.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Hana: Alex, you're taking over at noon. Nine June-close checks are complete; the maintenance invoice is the one still awaiting review.
  2. Alex: Please give me the close status first, then the invoice details and the decision we are waiting for. I do not want the one open item hidden behind the completed count.
  3. Hana: It's $240, dated July 2, but the description says June service.
  4. Alex: Then we need to distinguish the invoice date from the service period. The printed July date should not make us overlook the fact that the description relates to June.
  5. Hana: The accountant needs to decide the posting period. I haven't treated the July date as an automatic answer.
  6. Alex: I will retain that cutoff question as unresolved. The amount, document date, and service description give the reviewer the facts without our substituting an assumed answer.
  7. Hana: There isn't an approved adjusting entry either. Knowing the amount doesn't mean the entry is ready.
  8. Alex: I will make the posting approval status explicit. Knowing the amount does not establish that the period treatment or any proposed entry has been approved.
  9. Hana: At noon you're taking over the follow-up, not the accountant's authority to approve the treatment.
  10. Alex: Understood. I become the follow-up owner, not the accounting approver. I will pursue the response and preserve the decision with its actual reviewer and approval status.
  11. Hana: Please retain June service beside July 2. A note saying only July invoice would lose the issue.
  12. Alex: I will retain the cutoff evidence together: two hundred forty dollars, July second invoice date, June service. None of those details should disappear in the summary.
  13. Hana: Leave one item open after the handoff. Changing its owner doesn't complete it.
  14. Alex: Correct. The open item stays visible. A change of owner is not a completed review, and nine out of ten must not become ten out of ten just because the handoff is done.
  15. Hana: When the accountant responds, distinguish a period decision, an entry approval, and a request for more evidence.
  16. Alex: I will preserve those distinctions in the accountant review result. A request for more evidence is not approval, and a period discussion should not be silently expanded into permission to post.
  17. Hana: For now, no adjustment is authorized by this handoff. We just need the actual question and support reviewed.
  18. Alex: Agreed. An adjusting entry has not been approved, so the handoff should not describe one as ready or already recorded. I will keep that limit attached to the item.
  19. Hana: Readback: nine complete, one open; $240 July 2 invoice for June service; accountant review pending; you follow up from noon.
  20. Alex: That is accurate. The handover time changes who pursues the response, not the unresolved accounting status. I will take the follow-up with all those facts intact.
Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Alex takes the maintenance-period query

A fictional noon handover concerns the final June close item.

  1. Bookkeeper: Nine June checks are complete. The maintenance invoice for two hundred forty dollars remains the open item.
  2. Alex: Is its invoice date missing, or is the question which period it belongs to?
  3. Bookkeeper: It is dated July second and describes June service. The posting period needs the accountant's review.
  4. Alex: Has anyone already approved an adjusting entry that I need to follow through?
  5. Bookkeeper: No adjustment is approved. Please take over the query without treating either date as the accounting decision.
  6. Alex: I accept the follow-up. Where are the invoice and the note showing that review is still pending?
  7. Bookkeeper: They're linked under maintenance in the close checklist. The service description is visible on the invoice.
  8. Alex: I'll give the accountant both dates and ask for the posting-period decision.
  9. Bookkeeper: Thank you. Until that outcome is recorded, the checklist should stay at nine completed checks.
  10. Alex: Agreed. I own follow-up from noon; the accountant decides treatment.
A balanced trial balance with a missing reference

A fictional assistant asks a reviewer whether equal totals settle an open close question.

  1. Accounts assistant: The trial balance totals agree. Can I close the equipment query without its invoice reference?
  2. Close reviewer: Matching debit and credit totals don't establish that each entry has proper support.
  3. Accounts assistant: The amount and posting date are present, but I can't trace the equipment entry to its invoice.
  4. Close reviewer: Then the supporting-reference check remains open. It is a different question from whether the totals balance.
  5. Accounts assistant: Who should receive it now? The buyer holds the purchase file and can search the reference.
  6. Close reviewer: Please hand that search to the buyer, with the amount, date, and equipment description already available.
  7. Accounts assistant: I'll ask the buyer to accept the search, not simply copy them on an email.
  8. Close reviewer: Good. Keep the trial balance result as completed while leaving the document query visible.
  9. Accounts assistant: So our update distinguishes a balanced listing from a complete set of supporting records.
  10. Close reviewer: Exactly. Both findings matter, and the missing reference has a concrete next owner.
A schedule total agrees after one item is added

A fictional bookkeeper hands a corrected supporting schedule to a reviewer.

  1. Bookkeeper: The supplier schedule now agrees with the ledger after I included the omitted sixty-dollar item.
  2. Reviewer: Was the item missing from the ledger too, or only from the supporting schedule?
  3. Bookkeeper: Only from the schedule. The ledger already contained it under invoice L6 before today's correction.
  4. Reviewer: Then we shouldn't describe this as a new posting or an adjusting entry to the accounts.
  5. Bookkeeper: Correct. I revised the schedule total and linked L6 so you can check the change.
  6. Reviewer: Have you kept the earlier schedule version so the review trail explains the original mismatch?
  7. Bookkeeper: Yes, version one remains marked superseded. Version two includes the extra line and corrected total.
  8. Reviewer: I'll take the schedule review now. Does anything else remain open for this account?
  9. Bookkeeper: The document review is yours; I haven't identified another difference, but your check remains pending.
  10. Reviewer: Understood. I'm accepting the corrected schedule for review, not retrospectively treating the first version as accurate.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Nine checks done, one period question open
  1. Complete the period-close handoff.
  2. Read $240, July 2 invoice date, June service, accountant review pending, and Alex taking follow-up at noon. Then read the unapproved-entry status.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Hand over the period question without closing it

Complete the close handoff with the amount, service period, review owner, and open status preserved.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Bookkeeper: "The maintenance invoice is for ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

A. 240

Two hundred forty is the stated amount of the invoice awaiting period review.

2. Colleague: "Its description refers to ___ service."

Answer and explanation

C. June

June is the service period, despite the July 2 document date.

3. Bookkeeper: "The ___ must review the posting period."

Answer and explanation

A. accountant

Accountant is the required reviewer, not automatically the follow-up owner.

4. Colleague: "One checklist item remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. open

Open preserves the unfinished review even after Alex takes responsibility for follow-up.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 07: Handing over a period-close review
Original fictional case: The fictional June close has nine of ten checklist items complete. A $240 maintenance invoice is dated July 2 but describes June service. The accountant must review its posting period; no adjusting entry has been approved. Alex takes over the open-item follow-up from Hana at noon. The handoff must distinguish document date from service period and follow-up responsibility from authority to choose or post an accounting entry.
Language workshop: Nine checks done, one period question open
Communication goal: Hand over an unresolved cutoff question with its amount, dates, review owner, and unapproved posting status intact.
Language guidance: The invoice amount is $240, its date is July 2, and its description refers to June service. Those are distinct facts. The accountant must review the posting period; the assistant should not choose July solely from the invoice date.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Nine of ten checklist items are complete. / The maintenance invoice still needs period review. / The invoice is for $240. / It is dated July 2. / The description refers to June service. / The invoice date and service period are not the same fact. / The accountant needs to review the posting period. / No adjusting entry has been approved. / I will not assign July merely from the invoice date. / Alex takes over the open-item follow-up at noon. / Owning the follow-up does not mean approving the entry. / Retain the invoice and its June-service description. / The close is not ten out of ten yet. / This item remains open pending the accountant's review. / Please record the reviewed period and actual approval status. / Nine complete, one open, Alex following up, no entry approved.
Vocabulary: period close: Process of completing accounting work for a defined reporting interval. / close checklist: List of required tasks for finishing the reporting period. / open item: Matter still unresolved or incomplete. / invoice date: Date printed or assigned to the supplier document. / service period: Interval during which the invoiced service was provided. / posting period: Accounting period to which an entry is assigned. / cutoff question: Issue about which period should contain a transaction or amount. / maintenance expense: Cost associated with relevant maintenance work. / accountant review: Evaluation by the relevant accounting professional. / adjusting entry: Accounting entry addressing the relevant period-end recognition or measurement. / accrual: Recognition of an amount in the relevant period before the associated cash settlement. / accrued expense: Expense recognized with a related obligation before payment. / prepayment: Payment made before the related benefit or service is fully received. / supporting invoice: Supplier bill retained as evidence for the review. / posting approval: Permission for the specified accounting entry. / unposted item: Record or proposed transaction not yet entered in the ledger. / journal entry: Record of a transaction using the relevant accounting accounts. / general ledger: Main accounting record containing account balances and entries. / follow-up owner: Person responsible for pursuing the next response or action. / handover time: Time responsibility passes to the next person. / close status: Current completion stage of the period-end process. / carrying amount: Amount at which an asset or liability is recorded after relevant recognized adjustments. / recognition schedule: Record allocating an amount to accounting periods under the specified treatment. / cutoff evidence: Dates and service information relevant to the period question.
Speaking task: Complete the period-close handoff. Read $240, July 2 invoice date, June service, accountant review pending, and Alex taking follow-up at noon. Then read the unapproved-entry status. Complete the transfer and check the key. Keep nine complete and one open even after the handoff.
Partner: Hana: Outgoing bookkeeper / Alex: Incoming bookkeeper

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

08Correcting records while preserving the trailA duplicate entry, not a recovered payment+

Your goal

Correct an overstatement by distinguishing a confirmed tracker duplication from unposted ledger changes and unproven cash movements.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

A duplicate entry, not a recovered payment

In fictional tracker version 3, invoice J14 for $180 appears twice because the same file was imported twice. The controller has confirmed one duplicate entry. No ledger correction has been posted. A previous message wrongly said the duplicate payment had been recovered, but no duplicate payment is established. Bookkeeper Rosa must correct the message with controller Ellis and preserve a traceable record of the verified issue and any later authorized change.

Rosa: Bookkeeper / Ellis: Controller

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What has the controller confirmed?

Answer and explanation

B. One duplicate tracker entry for J14

The verified issue is a duplicate entry caused by importing the same file twice.

2. What is the current ledger-correction status?

Answer and explanation

C. No correction has been posted

The supplied facts explicitly state that no ledger correction has been posted.

3. Which earlier claim is wrong?

Answer and explanation

C. The duplicate payment had been recovered

No duplicate payment is established, so the recovery statement is unsupported.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

J14 is a $180 invoice shown twice in tracker version 3 because the same file was imported twice. Two displayed entries would total $360, with one extra $180 entry. That tracker arithmetic does not establish two payments, a $180 cash loss, or a recovery.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
tracker
Working record used to monitor items or statuses.identify the tracker
tracker version
Number or label distinguishing a revision of the working record.specify tracker version
duplicate entry
Repeated record of the same underlying item.confirm a duplicate entry
duplicate import
Repeated loading of the same source file or data.identify a duplicate import
source file
Original file from which data were loaded.preserve the source file
import log
Record of data-loading events and their status.consult the import log
controller
Person overseeing the relevant accounting controls and reporting.obtain controller confirmation
verified finding
Conclusion supported by the stated review.report the verified finding
ledger correction
Authorized change to the accounting record.distinguish a ledger correction
posted correction
Change actually entered in the ledger through the relevant process.verify a posted correction
duplicate payment
Actual payment made more than once for the same obligation.distinguish a duplicate payment
payment recovery
Return or recovery of money previously paid.verify payment recovery
cash movement
Actual transfer of money into or out of an account.confirm cash movement
status correction
Amendment of an inaccurate progress or outcome statement.issue a status correction
retraction
Explicit withdrawal of a previous unsupported claim.make a precise retraction
correction trail
Record linking the original issue, evidence, and authorized change.preserve the correction trail
record retention
Keeping information under the applicable requirements and process.follow record-retention procedures
change authorization
Permission for a specified amendment.obtain change authorization
transaction reference
Identifier connecting records to the relevant business item.retain the transaction reference
working total
Sum calculated from a working record rather than a verified final ledger.qualify a working total
overstatement
Claim or figure that exceeds what the evidence supports.correct an overstatement
cross-reference
Link between related records or messages.include a cross-reference
correction owner
Person responsible for carrying out the approved amendment.identify the correction owner
resolution evidence
Information demonstrating that the specific issue has been resolved.request resolution evidence

The controller confirmed one duplicate entry, but no ledger correction has been posted. Withdraw the earlier recovery claim explicitly. Preserve the original reference, verified finding, and later authorized correction trail; do not silently erase the history or invent a cash transaction.

C Notice the language

Correct the strongest false claim, not just the smallest number

Changing a tracker count does not repair a message claiming that money was recovered. Name the earlier statement, withdraw it, and replace it with the verified finding. Preserve the distinction between a file import, a ledger posting, and a cash transaction.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the record
This concerns tracker version 3 and invoice J14.
State the invoice amount
J14 is for $180.
Describe the duplication
The same file was imported twice.
Name the verified finding
The controller confirmed one duplicate entry.
Keep tracker and ledger separate
A tracker duplicate is not automatically a posted ledger duplicate.
State posting status
No ledger correction has been posted.
Separate record and payment
A repeated entry does not prove a repeated payment.
Own the earlier error
My previous message said the duplicate payment had been recovered.
Retract the claim
That recovery statement was unsupported and is withdrawn.
State the evidence limit
No duplicate payment has been established.
Avoid invented cash movement
We have not confirmed any recovered funds.
Preserve the trail
Keep the original record and correction history linked.
Request authorization
Any amendment needs the appropriate approval.
Make the correction visible
I will send an explicit correction to the earlier recipients.
Report only the actual stage
The duplicate entry is confirmed; the ledger correction is not posted.
Close with a precise status
One tracker duplicate, no established duplicate payment, no posted ledger correction.
6 language and precision notes
Appears twice
Describes the working record without asserting two underlying transactions.
Confirmed one duplicate
Specifies the verified issue and count.
Not automatically
Blocks an unsupported inference between different systems or stages.
Was unsupported and is withdrawn
Corrects the earlier claim explicitly.
No ... established
Limits the evidence without inventing a contrary event.
Keep ... linked
Preserves traceability between the issue and its correction.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which correction is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. One J14 tracker duplicate is confirmed; no duplicate payment or recovery is established

The statement preserves the verified record issue and withdraws the unsupported cash claim.

2. What does twice $180 establish here?

Answer and explanation

A. The duplicate tracker rows sum to $360

The calculation concerns two working-record entries, not proven payment or ledger events.

3. Which action preserves the trail?

Answer and explanation

A. Link the explicit correction to the original message and supporting records

A traceable correction retains the connection between the error, evidence, and authorized amendment.

4. Which status remains uncompleted?

Answer and explanation

B. Ledger correction posting

The supplied facts confirm the duplicate but explicitly leave ledger correction unposted.

E Conversations

A duplicate entry, not a recovered payment

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Rosa: Bookkeeper / Ellis: Controller

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • correction trail
  • duplicate entry
  • status correction
  • working total
  • retraction
  • payment recovery
  • Change authorization
  • import log
  • verified finding
  • posted correction
  1. Rosa
    Ellis, my J14 update said a duplicate payment had been recovered. I need to withdraw that: the evidence only establishes a repeated tracker entry.
  2. Ellis
    That's right. The 1 is one duplicate J14 entry in tracker version 3 because the same file was imported twice.
  3. Rosa
    It's a $180 invoice showing twice. I jumped from two rows to two payments, which wasn't justified.
  4. Ellis
    A 2 isn't proof of another payment. The correction needs to address that cash claim, not merely change the number of rows.
  5. Rosa
    The two rows sum to $360 in the tracker. That doesn't show $360 leaving the bank.
  6. Ellis
    Correct. It's a 3 from the duplicated rows, not a verified bank or ledger balance. The extra $180 displayed isn't an established cash loss.
  7. Rosa
    No ledger change has been posted either. I shouldn't say the accounts are fixed because you've identified the import problem.
  8. Ellis
    Exactly. A 4 hasn't occurred. Confirming the issue, authorizing a change, and posting it are different stages.
  9. Rosa
    I'll send the same recipients a correction. How do I make sure the recovery claim doesn't remain the headline?
  10. Ellis
    Lead with a direct 5: the statement that a duplicate payment was recovered was unsupported and is withdrawn. Then state the verified finding.
  11. Rosa
    One duplicate J14 entry in version 3, same source file imported twice, and no duplicate payment established.
  12. Ellis
    Yes. Don't imply a 6 either. Without evidence of a duplicate payment, we can't report recovered funds as an accomplished result.
  13. Rosa
    Should I remove the original message so nobody relies on it later?
  14. Ellis
    Preserve the 7 through the actual records process. Link the explicit correction to the earlier message so readers can see what replaces the false claim.
  15. Rosa
    The source file and import information should remain available too, rather than disappearing during a tracker cleanup.
  16. Ellis
    Correct. The 8 helps support how the duplication happened. Keep it connected to the review and any authorized amendment.
  17. Rosa
    Sending this correction doesn't give me permission to alter the ledger, so I'll keep those actions separate.
  18. Ellis
    Yes. 9 must cover the specific amendment. A corrected message doesn't approve or post an accounting entry.
  19. Rosa
    My final wording will say one confirmed tracker duplicate, no established duplicate payment or recovery, and no ledger correction posted.
  20. Ellis
    That's the accurate 10. Retain J14, version 3, and the supporting record, then report later resolution only when its evidence exists.

Answers and explanations
  1. verified finding Turn 2. Verified finding is one duplicate J14 entry in tracker version 3, not a recovered payment.
  2. duplicate entry Turn 4. Duplicate entry is a repeated record of J14 and does not prove a second actual payment.
  3. working total Turn 6. Working total is the sum of the duplicate tracker rows, not a verified cash or ledger balance.
  4. posted correction Turn 8. Posted correction would be an actual ledger change, and none has occurred in the supplied status.
  5. retraction Turn 10. Retraction explicitly withdraws the unsupported claim that a duplicate payment was recovered.
  6. payment recovery Turn 12. Payment recovery would require evidence that money was returned, which is not established here.
  7. correction trail Turn 14. Correction trail links the original false statement, explicit correction, and evidence rather than silently erasing history.
  8. import log Turn 16. Import log can support the confirmed repeated file import and should remain connected to the review.
  9. Change authorization Turn 18. Change authorization is required for the actual amendment and is not created by correcting a message.
  10. status correction Turn 20. Status correction replaces the unsupported recovery claim with the verified duplicate-entry finding and unresolved ledger status.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Rosa: Ellis, my J14 update said a duplicate payment had been recovered. I need to withdraw that: the evidence only establishes a repeated tracker entry.
  2. Ellis: That's right. The verified finding is one duplicate J14 entry in tracker version 3 because the same file was imported twice.
  3. Rosa: It's a $180 invoice showing twice. I jumped from two rows to two payments, which wasn't justified.
  4. Ellis: A duplicate entry isn't proof of another payment. The correction needs to address that cash claim, not merely change the number of rows.
  5. Rosa: The two rows sum to $360 in the tracker. That doesn't show $360 leaving the bank.
  6. Ellis: Correct. It's a working total from the duplicated rows, not a verified bank or ledger balance. The extra $180 displayed isn't an established cash loss.
  7. Rosa: No ledger change has been posted either. I shouldn't say the accounts are fixed because you've identified the import problem.
  8. Ellis: Exactly. A posted correction hasn't occurred. Confirming the issue, authorizing a change, and posting it are different stages.
  9. Rosa: I'll send the same recipients a correction. How do I make sure the recovery claim doesn't remain the headline?
  10. Ellis: Lead with a direct retraction: the statement that a duplicate payment was recovered was unsupported and is withdrawn. Then state the verified finding.
  11. Rosa: One duplicate J14 entry in version 3, same source file imported twice, and no duplicate payment established.
  12. Ellis: Yes. Don't imply a payment recovery either. Without evidence of a duplicate payment, we can't report recovered funds as an accomplished result.
  13. Rosa: Should I remove the original message so nobody relies on it later?
  14. Ellis: Preserve the correction trail through the actual records process. Link the explicit correction to the earlier message so readers can see what replaces the false claim.
  15. Rosa: The source file and import information should remain available too, rather than disappearing during a tracker cleanup.
  16. Ellis: Correct. The import log helps support how the duplication happened. Keep it connected to the review and any authorized amendment.
  17. Rosa: Sending this correction doesn't give me permission to alter the ledger, so I'll keep those actions separate.
  18. Ellis: Yes. Change authorization must cover the specific amendment. A corrected message doesn't approve or post an accounting entry.
  19. Rosa: My final wording will say one confirmed tracker duplicate, no established duplicate payment or recovery, and no ledger correction posted.
  20. Ellis: That's the accurate status correction. Retain J14, version 3, and the supporting record, then report later resolution only when its evidence exists.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

An entry was duplicated, not a payment confirmed

A fictional clerk corrects a message about J14 in tracker version three.

  1. Clerk: I need to correct my earlier message. I called J14 a recovered duplicate payment, which wasn't established.
  2. Controller: What's confirmed, and what's unknown about the one-hundred-eighty-dollar item?
  3. Clerk: You confirmed a duplicate invoice entry in tracker version three. We haven't established duplicate cash movement.
  4. Controller: That's right. Has a ledger correction been posted since we last discussed the tracker?
  5. Clerk: No. The ledger review remains pending. My message overstated both the payment finding and the recovery.
  6. Controller: Please correct that with the same recipients so they don't keep repeating the stronger claim.
  7. Clerk: I will say duplicate entry confirmed, duplicate payment unestablished, and ledger correction pending your review.
  8. Controller: Include the original entry reference. A reader should be able to follow what we are actually correcting.
  9. Clerk: I've linked J14 and version three. The correction addresses the claim as well as the amount.
  10. Controller: Thank you. Keep the record issue separate from any payment tracing that may follow.
The correction email pointed to the old file

A fictional bookkeeper repairs a version-link mistake with a colleague.

  1. Colleague: Your message says corrected, but the attachment still duplicates invoice M8.
  2. Bookkeeper: You're right. I linked version two instead of version three, which contains the reviewed tracker correction.
  3. Colleague: So I wasn't looking at the current version. Can you identify precisely what changed between them?
  4. Bookkeeper: Version three removes the repeated tracker line and links the original transaction.
  5. Colleague: Does that mean the ledger was changed as well, or is this only the tracker?
  6. Bookkeeper: Only the tracker. The ledger had one entry, so no ledger correction was needed.
  7. Colleague: Thanks. Please send the correct link and label the earlier attachment superseded rather than letting both seem current.
  8. Bookkeeper: I've corrected the link now. The message explicitly identifies version three and the scope of the change.
  9. Colleague: I see the note and single line. That resolves my version confusion.
  10. Bookkeeper: I'll leave version two marked superseded in the version history, preserving the audit trail.
A proposed reversal is still only a proposal

A fictional clerk clarifies the status of an accounting correction during a review call.

  1. Reviewer: The query says reversal prepared. Has the original entry already been offset?
  2. Accounts clerk: No. Prepared refers to the draft correction request; the proposed reversal has not been posted.
  3. Reviewer: I read it as completed because the task was marked done in the document-preparation column.
  4. Accounts clerk: That label was too broad. I'll identify preparation as complete and accounting approval as pending.
  5. Reviewer: Please also confirm which original transaction the proposal refers to, since two references look similar.
  6. Accounts clerk: It refers to entry six eighteen for eighty dollars, not entry six eighty for eighteen dollars.
  7. Reviewer: Good distinction. Reading those references aloud without the amounts could easily cause another mix-up.
  8. Accounts clerk: I'll state both reference and amount together, and keep the original entry linked in the request.
  9. Reviewer: Then I can review the intended correction without assuming anything has already changed in the ledger.
  10. Accounts clerk: Exactly. Draft prepared for entry six eighteen, eighty dollars; approval and posting remain separate, unfinished stages.

Lesson 08: Correcting records while preserving the trail
Original fictional case: In fictional tracker version 3, invoice J14 for $180 appears twice because the same file was imported twice. The controller has confirmed one duplicate entry. No ledger correction has been posted. A previous message wrongly said the duplicate payment had been recovered, but no duplicate payment is established. Bookkeeper Rosa must correct the message with controller Ellis and preserve a traceable record of the verified issue and any later authorized change.
Language workshop: A duplicate entry, not a recovered payment
Communication goal: Correct an overstatement by distinguishing a confirmed tracker duplication from unposted ledger changes and unproven cash movements.
Language guidance: J14 is a $180 invoice shown twice in tracker version 3 because the same file was imported twice. Two displayed entries would total $360, with one extra $180 entry. That tracker arithmetic does not establish two payments, a $180 cash loss, or a recovery.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): This concerns tracker version 3 and invoice J14. / J14 is for $180. / The same file was imported twice. / The controller confirmed one duplicate entry. / A tracker duplicate is not automatically a posted ledger duplicate. / No ledger correction has been posted. / A repeated entry does not prove a repeated payment. / My previous message said the duplicate payment had been recovered. / That recovery statement was unsupported and is withdrawn. / No duplicate payment has been established. / We have not confirmed any recovered funds. / Keep the original record and correction history linked. / Any amendment needs the appropriate approval. / I will send an explicit correction to the earlier recipients. / The duplicate entry is confirmed; the ledger correction is not posted. / One tracker duplicate, no established duplicate payment, no posted ledger correction.
Vocabulary: tracker: Working record used to monitor items or statuses. / tracker version: Number or label distinguishing a revision of the working record. / duplicate entry: Repeated record of the same underlying item. / duplicate import: Repeated loading of the same source file or data. / source file: Original file from which data were loaded. / import log: Record of data-loading events and their status. / controller: Person overseeing the relevant accounting controls and reporting. / verified finding: Conclusion supported by the stated review. / ledger correction: Authorized change to the accounting record. / posted correction: Change actually entered in the ledger through the relevant process. / duplicate payment: Actual payment made more than once for the same obligation. / payment recovery: Return or recovery of money previously paid. / cash movement: Actual transfer of money into or out of an account. / status correction: Amendment of an inaccurate progress or outcome statement. / retraction: Explicit withdrawal of a previous unsupported claim. / correction trail: Record linking the original issue, evidence, and authorized change. / record retention: Keeping information under the applicable requirements and process. / change authorization: Permission for a specified amendment. / transaction reference: Identifier connecting records to the relevant business item. / working total: Sum calculated from a working record rather than a verified final ledger. / overstatement: Claim or figure that exceeds what the evidence supports. / cross-reference: Link between related records or messages. / correction owner: Person responsible for carrying out the approved amendment. / resolution evidence: Information demonstrating that the specific issue has been resolved.
Speaking task: Complete the tracker-correction dialogue. Read Rosa's retraction and the replacement finding aloud. Distinguish two $180 tracker rows from actual payments or recovered funds. Complete the transfer and check the key. Repeat the status with no posted ledger correction and the original references retained.
Partner: Rosa: Bookkeeper / Ellis: Controller
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the corrected status message. Distinguish the tracker, invoice amount, and unproven payment claim.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Rosa: Sending this correction doesn't give me permission to alter the ledger, so I'll keep those actions separate.
Ellis: Yes. Change authorization must cover the specific amendment. A corrected message doesn't approve or post an accounting entry.
Rosa: My final wording will say one confirmed tracker duplicate, no established duplicate payment or recovery, and no ledger correction posted.
Ellis: That's the accurate status correction. Retain J14, version 3, and the supporting record, then report later resolution only when its evidence exists.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
